<!-- theorema-1: body_md -->
Let \((S,M)\) and \((S',M')\) be surfaces with stops of genus at least
two, with at least one stop on every boundary component. An abstract group
isomorphism
\[
 \operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\simeq\operatorname{Mod}(S',M')
\]
implies that \((S,M)\) and \((S',M')\) are isomorphic as surfaces with
stops.
<!-- end theorema-1 -->
<!-- theoremb-1: body_md -->
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta), \mathbb S'=(S',M',\eta')\) be graded surfaces with stops of genus at least two whose partially wrapped Fukaya categories are smooth and proper.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item
If \(D(\mathbb S)\simeq D(\mathbb S')\), then \(g(S)=g(S')\), and \(|\pi_0(\partial S)|=|\pi_0(\partial S')|\).
\item A shift-preserving isomorphism
\(D(\mathbb S)\simeq D(\mathbb S')\) implies \((S,\eta)\simeq(S',\eta')\).
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theoremb-1 -->
<!-- theoremc-1: body_md -->
For every \(g\geq2\), the invariant \(\mathfrak D\) has a countably
infinite fibre on graded surfaces with stops of genus \(g\). The surfaces
in this fibre have the same underlying graded surface, while their smooth
and proper partially wrapped Fukaya categories are pairwise non-equivalent.
Moreover, for every \(R\geq1\), some fibre contains more than \(R\) such
categories with the same Grothendieck rank.
<!-- end theoremc-1 -->
<!-- theoremd-1: body_md -->
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) and
\(\mathbb S'=(S',M',\eta')\) be graded surfaces with stops whose
partially wrapped Fukaya categories are smooth and proper. Suppose that
\(\eta\) and \(\eta'\) are induced by nowhere-vanishing vector fields.
An isomorphism of their rational Grothendieck groups which intertwines
the Serre actions implies \((S,M)\simeq(S',M')\).
<!-- end theoremd-1 -->
<!-- theoreme-1: body_md -->
For graded surfaces with stops of genus at least two whose partially
wrapped Fukaya categories are smooth and proper, the invariant
\(\mathfrak R\) determines the underlying graded surface and the total
number of stops. Let \(b=|\pi_0(\partial S)|\), and let \(t\) be the
number of boundary components with odd winding. Its fibre on isomorphism
classes of graded surfaces with stops satisfies
\[
 \#\mathfrak R^{-1}([\mathfrak R(\mathbb S)])\leq
 \begin{cases}
 4,&b=4,\ t=4,\\
 2,&b=2,\ \text{or }(b=3,\ t=2),\\
 1,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
In particular, \(\mathfrak R\) determines the complete graded surface
with stops when \(b=1\) or \(b\geq5\).
<!-- end theoreme-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:line-field-classification}
Let \(S\) be a compact connected oriented surface with non-empty
boundary. A line field \(\eta\) is induced by a nowhere-vanishing vector
field if and only if all its winding numbers are even. The orbits of
line fields under orientation-preserving mapping classes fixing the
boundary pointwise are classified as follows.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item In genus zero, two line fields are homotopic if and only if their
boundary winding numbers agree componentwise.
\item In genus one, two line fields lie in the same orbit if and only if
their boundary winding numbers and their invariants
\(\widetilde{\mathcal A}\) agree.
\item In genus at least two, two line fields lie in the same orbit if
and only if their boundary winding numbers and their
\(\sigma\)-invariants agree, and their Arf invariants agree whenever
these are defined.
\end{enumerate}
The corresponding statement for graded surfaces with stops also
requires the numbers of stops on matched boundary components to agree.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
A graded mapping class of \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) is an isotopy class
of pairs \((f,[h])\), where \(f\) represents an element of
\(\operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\) and \([h]\) is the homotopy class of a path from
\(f^*\eta\) to \(\eta\) in the space of line fields.  The resulting group
is denoted by \(\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\).
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
The forgetful homomorphism from the graded mapping class group has image
\(H_\eta\) and fits into a central extension
\begin{equation}\label{eq:grading-extension}
 1\longrightarrow\mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle
 \longrightarrow\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)
 \longrightarrow H_\eta\longrightarrow1.
\end{equation}
Here \([1]\) is the positive deck translation of the grading cover and
corresponds to the categorical shift.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
A path from \(f^*\eta\) to \(\eta\) determines a \(\mathbb Z\)-equivariant lift
of the map on \(\mathbb P(TS)\) induced by \(Df\).  Conversely, such a
lift determines the homotopy class of this path.  Hence a graded lift
exists precisely when \(f\in H_\eta\).  Any two lifts of the same \(f\)
differ by a unique deck translation. Therefore, the kernel is 
\(\mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle\). Since deck translations commute with every
\(\mathbb Z\)-equivariant lift, the extension is central.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:stopped-stabilizer-root-presentation}
Let \(H_\eta^0\) be the subgroup of \(H_\eta\) preserving every
boundary component, and put
\(F=\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S,\partial S)}(\eta)\). If
\(m_i=|M\cap B_i|\), then
\[
 H_\eta^0\cong
 \left\langle F,\rho_1,\ldots,\rho_b\ \middle|\
 [\rho_i,F]=1,\ [\rho_i,\rho_j]=1,\
 \rho_i^{m_i}=T_i\right\rangle .
\]
Consequently,
\[
 H_\eta^0/F\cong\prod_{i=1}^b\mathbb Z/m_i\mathbb Z.
\]
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Let \(h\in H_\eta^0\). Since \(h\) preserves each boundary component \(B_i\), it induces a cyclic permutation of the stops \(M\cap B_i\). Hence there exist integers \(a_i\) such that
\(
h'= h\,\rho_1^{a_1}\cdots \rho_b^{a_b}\)
fixes every stop.

On each \(B_i\), the boundary restriction of \(h'\) is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of the circle fixing all points of \(M\cap B_i\). It can therefore be isotoped to the identity relative to these stops. After an isotopy supported in a collar of the boundary, we may assume that \(h'\) is the identity on \(\partial S\). Collar isotopies do not change the absolute homotopy class of the line field, so the adjusted \(h'\) still lies in \(H_\eta\). Thus \(h'\in F\), which implies that \(H_\eta^0\) is generated by \(F\) together with \(\rho_1,\ldots,\rho_b\).

The rotations \(\rho_i\) are central in the boundary-preserving subgroup, and \(\rho_i^{m_i}=T_i\) is the full boundary twist on \(B_i\). Therefore
\[
[\rho_i,F]=1,\qquad [\rho_i,\rho_j]=1,\qquad \rho_i^{m_i}=T_i.
\]
Conversely, every element of the group on the right can be written as
\[
f\,\rho_1^{a_1}\cdots \rho_b^{a_b},\qquad f\in F,
\]
and the exponents \(a_i\) are well defined only modulo \(m_i\), because \(\rho_i^{m_i}=T_i\in F\). Hence these relations give the required presentation.

Finally, the standard inclusion of the boundary-pointwise mapping class group into the stopped mapping class group is injective. Therefore \(F\) is exactly the subgroup of \(H_\eta^0\) consisting of elements that act trivially on the stops on every boundary component. The quotient \(H_\eta^0/F\) therefore records only the cyclic permutations of the \(m_i\) stops on each \(B_i\), so
\[
H_\eta^0/F\cong \prod_{i=1}^b \mathbb Z/m_i\mathbb Z.
\]
This proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:complete-surface-invariant}
Let \(A\) and \(A'\) be homologically smooth and proper graded gentle
algebras, with surface models \((S,M,\eta)\) and
\((S',M',\eta')\).  Then \(\operatorname{per}(A)\) and \(\operatorname{per}(A')\) are triangle
equivalent if and only if there is an orientation-preserving homeomorphism
\[
 f\colon(S,M)\longrightarrow(S',M')
\]
such that \(f^*\eta'\simeq\eta\).
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:opper-geometrization}
Let \(A\) be a connected homologically smooth and proper graded
gentle algebra over \(\mathbf{k}\), with surface model
\(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\). Assume that every boundary component
contains a stop and that \(\chi(S)<0\).
Under the Morita identification
\(D(\mathbb S)\simeq\operatorname{DPic}(A)\), there is a split exact sequence
\[
 1\longrightarrow N(\mathbb S)
 \longrightarrow D(\mathbb S)
 \xrightarrow{\,\Psi_{\mathbb S}\,}\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)
 \longrightarrow1,
\]
where
\[
 N(\mathbb S)\simeq
 \mathbb G_a^{\phi_A(1,1)}\rtimes H^1(S;\mathbf{k}^\times).
\]
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:geometric-serre-functor}
Suppose that the surface category is smooth and proper. Under the
surface model, the Auslander--Reiten translation \(\mathsf S[-1]\)
of a perfect arc object is obtained by moving both endpoints to the
adjacent points of \(M^\circ\) in the negative boundary direction
(with the surface on the right) and using the induced grading.
The Serre functor is obtained by following this graded endpoint
move with the shift \([1]\).
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:charge-lattice}
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) be a compact graded surface with stops, and let
\(\mathbb Z_\eta\) be the orientation local system of the line field.
There is a natural isomorphism
\[
 K(\mathbb S)
 \simeq H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Z_\eta).
\]
If \(\eta=\mathbb R\xi\) is induced by a vector framing, then
\(\mathbb Z_\eta\) is trivial and the right-hand side is
\(H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Z)\).
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
Assume that \(\eta=\mathbb R\xi\) is induced by a vector framing.
Let \(R\) be simultaneous positive one-step boundary rotation,
represented in boundary collars so that it moves both \(M\) and
\(M^\circ\) to the next points of the same type in the positive
boundary direction. Under the identification
\(K(\mathbb S)\simeq H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Z)\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:serre-rotation-action}
 (\mathsf S[-1])_*=\varepsilon R_*^{-1},
 \qquad \varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}.
\end{equation}
Here \(\varepsilon\) is the sign induced by the graded lift on the
orientation local system after trivializing it using \(\xi\).
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
By Proposition~\ref{prop:geometric-serre-functor}, the functor
\(\mathsf S[-1]\) acts on the generating graded arcs by a single
graded lift of \(R^{-1}\). The charge-lattice identification is
natural for graded diffeomorphisms, including the induced
isomorphism of orientation local systems. The vector field \(\xi\)
trivializes \(\mathbb Z_\eta\), and in this trivialization the induced
isomorphism of rank-one integral local systems is multiplication
by a single sign \(\varepsilon\), since \(S\) is connected. Thus its
action on relative homology is \(\varepsilon R_*^{-1}\). The classes
of the graded arcs generate \(K(\mathbb S)\), which proves
\eqref{eq:serre-rotation-action}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:ungraded-reconstruction}
Let \((S,M)\) and \((S',M')\) be compact connected oriented
surfaces with stops and non-empty boundary, at least one stop on every boundary
component, and genera at least two.  If
\[
 \operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\simeq\operatorname{Mod}(S',M')
\]
as abstract groups, then there is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\((S,M)\simeq(S',M')\).  In particular, the group determines the genus,
the number of boundary components, and the distribution of stops among
them.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:mapping-class-rigidity}
Let \(X\) be a closed oriented surface of genus at least two, and let
\(Q\neq\varnothing\) be finite.  If \(\Gamma\) is a finite-index subgroup
of \(\operatorname{Mod}^*(X,Q)\), then every injective homomorphism
\[
 \Gamma\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}^*(X,Q)
\]
is the restriction of conjugation by an element of
\(\operatorname{Mod}^*(X,Q)\).
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:intrinsic-capping-extension}
The subgroup \(A_M\) is isomorphic to \(\mathbb Z^b\). It is the unique maximal
normal abelian subgroup of \(G\), and hence is characteristic. Moreover,
\[
 G^0=C_G(A_M).
\]
Capping the boundary components induces a central extension
\[
 1\longrightarrow A_M\longrightarrow G^0
 \longrightarrow\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q)\longrightarrow1.
\]
Its kernel is precisely the subgroup generated by the one-step boundary
rotations. In particular,
\(
 A_M=Z(G^0)\).
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The rotations commute and satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary-root-relation}
 \rho_i^{m_i}=T_{B_i},
\end{equation}
where \(T_{B_i}\) is the positive boundary twist. For
\(g\geq2\), the boundary twists \(T_{B_i}\) are independent in
\(\operatorname{Mod}(S,\partial S)\), and \(\operatorname{Mod}(S,\partial S)\) embeds in
\(\operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\). Suppose that \(\prod_i\rho_i^{a_i}=1\), and choose a
positive common multiple \(m\) of \(m_1,\ldots,m_b\). Then
\[
  1=\left(\prod_i\rho_i^{a_i}\right)^m
   =\prod_i(\rho_i^{m_i})^{a_i m/m_i}
   =\prod_i T_{B_i}^{a_i m/m_i}.
\]
Independence gives \(a_i m/m_i=0\) for every \(i\), hence \(a_i=0\).
Thus \(A_M\cong\mathbb Z^b\).


We next show that \(A_M\) can be recognized from the abstract group
\(G\). The capping construction defines a homomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coloured-capping-map}
 \kappa:G\longrightarrow \operatorname{Mod}(\widehat S,Q).
\end{equation}
Its image consists of those mapping classes whose induced permutation of
\(Q\) preserves the function \(q_i\mapsto m_i\), and its kernel is
\(A_M\).  Indeed, when all boundary points are required to be fixed, this is the usual capping exact sequence, whose kernel is generated by the
boundary twists. Allowing cyclic permutations of the \(m_i\) stops on
\(B_i\) replaces the generator \(T_{B_i}\) by its distinguished
\(m_i\)-th root \(\rho_i\), and introduces no further element in the
kernel.

We claim that the image of \(\kappa\) has no nontrivial normal abelian
subgroup. Let \(L\) be such a subgroup and put
\(P=\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q)\). The group \(P\) is normal in
\(\operatorname{im}\kappa\) and has finite index there.
Lemma~\ref{lem:no-normal-abelian-subgroup} gives \(L\cap P=1\), so
\(L\) is finite. The kernel of the conjugation action of \(P\) on \(L\)
has finite index in \(P\). Since this kernel is centralized by \(L\),
the second assertion of
Lemma~\ref{lem:no-normal-abelian-subgroup} implies \(L=1\).

It follows that every normal abelian subgroup of \(G\) is contained in
\(\ker\kappa=A_M\). Hence \(A_M\) is the unique maximal normal abelian
subgroup of \(G\), and therefore is characteristic. Note that this
argument also covers the case \((g,b)=(2,1)\). In that case the puncture
removes the hyperelliptic centre that occurs for the closed genus-two
mapping class group.

The conjugation action of \(G\) on \(A_M\) agrees with its permutation
action on the boundary components. Indeed, an orientation-preserving mapping
class sends \(\rho_i\) to \(\rho_j\) precisely when it sends \(B_i\) to
\(B_j\). The boundary orientation rules out \(\rho_j^{-1}\). It follows that
\(
 G^0=C_G(A_M)
\)
is characteristic. Its elements are precisely the mapping classes that
preserve every boundary component. Restricting
\eqref{eq:coloured-capping-map} therefore gives the central extension
\begin{equation}\label{eq:marked-capping-extension}
 1\longrightarrow A_M
 \longrightarrow G^0
 \longrightarrow\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q)
 \longrightarrow1.
\end{equation}
The last arrow is onto.  Indeed, a pure mapping class of \((\widehat S,Q)\) may be
represented by a diffeomorphism which is the identity on small discs about
the points of \(Q\), and whose restriction to the complement fixes all
stops.  The quotient
\(\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q)\) is centre-free for \(g\geq2\) and \(b\geq1\)
\cite{ParisRolfsen}. Thus \(A_M=Z(G^0)\), which gives a second
description of both the kernel and the quotient in
\eqref{eq:marked-capping-extension}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:pure-mapping-class-ranks}
Let \(X\) be a closed surface of genus \(g\geq2\), and let \(Q\) be a
non-empty set of \(b\) punctures.  Then the virtual cohomological dimension of $\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)$ is
\[
\operatorname{vcd}\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)=4g-4+b,
\]
and the maximal rank of a free abelian
subgroup of $\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)$ is
\[\max\{n:\mathbb Z^n\hookrightarrow\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)\}=3g-3+b.
\]
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:pure-mapping-class-rigidity}
Let \(\widehat S\) and \(\widehat S'\) be closed oriented surfaces of
genera \(g,g'\geq2\), and let \(Q,Q'\) be non-empty finite sets of
cardinalities \(b,b'\).  Every abstract isomorphism
\[
 \operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q)\xrightarrow{\sim}
 \operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S',Q')
\]
is induced by an extended diffeomorphism
\((\widehat S,Q)\to(\widehat S',Q')\).  In particular,
\(g=g'\) and \(b=b'\).
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
The two numbers in Proposition~\ref{prop:pure-mapping-class-ranks}
are invariants of the abstract group. Their difference is \(g-1\),
and either formula then recovers \(b\). Hence an isomorphism forces
\(g=g'\) and \(b=b'\).

Choose an extended diffeomorphism between the two punctured surfaces
and use it to identify them. The given isomorphism becomes an injective
homomorphism from the finite-index subgroup \(\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q)\)
of \(\operatorname{Mod}^*(\widehat S,Q)\) into \(\operatorname{Mod}^*(\widehat S,Q)\).
Since \(b>0\), the exceptional closed surface of genus two does not
occur. Theorem~\ref{thm:mapping-class-rigidity} shows that this
homomorphism is induced by conjugation by an extended mapping class.
Composing with the preliminary identification proves the assertion.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:extension-recovers-stops}
Let \(G=\operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\) and \(G'=\operatorname{Mod}(S',M')\), where both surfaces have genus
at least two.  Every abstract isomorphism \(G\simeq G'\) identifies the
genera and the numbers of boundary components, and carries the stop
distribution of \((S,M)\) to that of \((S',M')\).
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:intrinsic-capping-extension}, an isomorphism
\(\Phi:G\to G'\) maps \(A_M\) onto \(A_{M'}\), maps \(G^0\) onto
\((G')^0\), and induces
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pure-capped-isomorphism}
 \overline\Phi:
 \operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q)\xrightarrow{\sim}
 \operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S',Q').
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem:pure-mapping-class-rigidity} shows directly that
\eqref{eq:pure-capped-isomorphism} is induced by an extended
diffeomorphism \((\widehat S,Q)\to(\widehat S',Q')\).  In particular, \(g(S)=g(S'), b=b'\), and, after reindexing, the induced map sends \(q_i\) to \(q_i'\).

It remains to recover the integers \(m_i\). Let
\(\psi_i\in H^2(\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q);\mathbb Z)\) be the cotangent-line
class at \(q_i\). We use the convention in which the central
extension obtained by replacing the punctures with parametrized
boundary circles has \(\psi_i\) as its \(i\)-th component. Put
\(E_\partial=\operatorname{Mod}(S,\partial S)\).  The pointwise-boundary capping sequence
has class
\(
 e_\partial=\sum_{i=1}^b\psi_i\otimes T_{B_i}
\)
and fits into the upper row of the pushout diagram
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
1 \ar[r] &
\displaystyle \bigoplus_{i=1}^{b} \mathbb Z\langle T_{B_i}\rangle
  \ar[r] \ar[d, hook] &
E_{\partial} \ar[r] \ar[d, hook] &
\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q) \ar[r] \ar[d, equal] &
1 \\[2pt]
1 \ar[r] &
A_M \ar[r] &
G^0 \ar[r] &
\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q) \ar[r] &
1
\end{tikzcd}
\]
The left vertical map is
\(
 T_{B_i}\longmapsto m_i\rho_i\)
by \eqref{eq:boundary-root-relation}.  The lower row is the pushout of the
upper row along this map.  Naturality of extension classes therefore gives
\begin{equation*}
 e_M=\sum_{i=1}^b m_i\psi_i\otimes\rho_i
 \in H^2\bigl(\operatorname{PMod}(\widehat S,Q);A_M\bigr).
\end{equation*}
Changing the convention for positive twists changes every sign
simultaneously and does not affect the positive integers recovered below.


By Lemma~\ref{lem:psi-classes} in
Section~\ref{appsubsec:capping-rigidity}, the classes
\(\psi_1,\ldots,\psi_b\) are linearly independent over \(\mathbb Q\).
We may therefore compare extension classes after tensoring the
coefficient lattice with \(\mathbb Q\). Any resulting equality of
lattice vectors holds integrally because \(A_M\) is torsion free.
Let \(\alpha:A_M\to A_{M'}\) be the restriction of \(\Phi\).  Naturality
of extension classes gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:extension-naturality}
 (\overline\Phi^*\otimes\alpha^{-1})(e_{M'})=e_M.
\end{equation}
The geometric description of \(\overline\Phi\) gives a permutation
\(\pi\in\mathfrak S_b\) and one sign
\(\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}\) such that
\(
 \overline\Phi^*\psi_{\pi(i)}'=\varepsilon\psi_i\). The sign is common to all \(i\), since it records whether the inducing
diffeomorphism preserves or reverses the orientation of the surface.
Comparing the independent \(\psi_i\)-coefficients in
\eqref{eq:extension-naturality} yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stop-number-coefficients}
 m_i\rho_i
 =\varepsilon m_{\pi(i)}'\alpha^{-1}(\rho_{\pi(i)}')
 \qquad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq b.
\end{equation}
Each \(\rho_i\) and each \(\alpha^{-1}(\rho_i')\) is primitive in the
free abelian group \(A_M\).  Equation~\eqref{eq:stop-number-coefficients} therefore implies
\(m_i=m_{\pi(i)}'\) for every \(i\).  This recovers the number of stops on each boundary component.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
By Proposition~\ref{prop:extension-recovers-stops}, the surfaces have the same
genus, the same number of boundary components, and the same stop
distribution.  The classification of compact connected oriented surfaces
with stops therefore gives an orientation-preserving
diffeomorphism \((S,M)\simeq(S',M')\).
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex:low-genus-ungraded-failure}
The genus assumption in Theorem~\ref{thm:ungraded-reconstruction} cannot be
removed.  For genus zero, let \(P=\Sigma_{0,3}\) be a pair of pants and
choose stop distributions \((1,2,3)\) and \((1,2,4)\).  In either case the
three boundary components are distinguished by their stop numbers.  The
mapping class group is therefore freely generated by the three one-step
boundary rotations and is isomorphic to \(\mathbb Z^3\).  The two surfaces with stops
are not diffeomorphic because their stop distributions are different.

For genus one, let \(M_m\) be a set of \(m\) stops on the boundary of
\(\Sigma_{1,1}\), and write \(G_m=\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{1,1},M_m)\).  Let \(X_m\) and \(Y_m\)
be the twists about two nonseparating curves meeting once, and let
\(\rho_m\) be the positive one-step boundary rotation.  The braid relation and the
two-chain relation give the presentation
\[
 G_m=
 \left\langle X_m,Y_m,\rho_m\ \middle|\
 \begin{aligned}
 X_mY_mX_m&=Y_mX_mY_m,\\
 [X_m,\rho_m]&=[Y_m,\rho_m]=1,\\
 (X_mY_m)^6&=\rho_m^m
 \end{aligned}
 \right\rangle
\]
(see \cite{FarbMargalit}).  There is an isomorphism 
\(G_m\longrightarrow G_{m+12}\) defined by
\[
 \rho_m\longmapsto\rho_{m+12},
 \qquad
 X_m\longmapsto X_{m+12}\rho_{m+12}^{-1},
 \qquad
 Y_m\longmapsto Y_{m+12}\rho_{m+12}^{-1}.
\]
Indeed, the central factors contribute \(\rho_{m+12}^{-12}\) to the
sixth power of \(X_mY_m\).  Taking \(m=1\) gives one-holed tori
with one and thirteen stops whose mapping class groups are isomorphic.
The surfaces with stops themselves are not diffeomorphic.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
Theorem~\ref{thm:ungraded-reconstruction} concerns the full ungraded
mapping class group.  The quotient supplied by the geometrisation sequence
in Theorem~\ref{thm:opper-geometrization} is instead
\(\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\), and after
dividing by the shift it is the line-field stabilizer \(H_\eta\), not
\(\operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\).  Thus the theorem cannot be applied directly to an abstract
derived Picard group.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:derived-reconstruction}
Let \(A\) and \(A'\) be connected homologically smooth and proper graded
gentle algebras over \(\mathbf{k}\).  Write their surface models as
\[
 \mathbb S_A=(S_A,M_A,\eta_A),
 \qquad
 \mathbb S_{A'}=(S_{A'},M_{A'},\eta_{A'}).
\]
Assume that every boundary component contains a stop.  We further assume
that both \(S_A\) and \(S_{A'}\) have genus at least two.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item An abstract isomorphism \(\operatorname{DPic}(A)\simeq\operatorname{DPic}(A')\) implies
\[
 g(S_A)=g(S_{A'}),\qquad
 |\pi_0(\partial S_A)|=|\pi_0(\partial S_{A'})|.
\]
\item If the isomorphism \(\Phi\colon\operatorname{DPic}(A)\to\operatorname{DPic}(A')\) satisfies
\(\Phi([1])=[1]\), then there is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\[
 f\colon S_A\longrightarrow S_{A'}
 \qquad\text{with}\qquad f^*\eta_{A'}\simeq\eta_A.
\]
\end{enumerate}
Thus the derived Picard group, together with the shift, determines
the underlying graded surface after the stops are forgotten.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:characteristic-geometrization-kernel}
Assume that \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) has genus at least two.  Then
\[
 \operatorname{DA}\bigl(D(\mathbb S)\bigr)=N(\mathbb S).
\]
Moreover, for every prime \(\ell\), there is a natural identification
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ell-local-systems}
 \operatorname{Tor}_{\ell^\infty}
 \bigl(N(\mathbb S)^{\mathrm{ab}}\bigr)
 \simeq H^1(S;\mu_{\ell^\infty}),
\end{equation}
and conjugation by \(D(\mathbb S)\) induces the action on rank-one local systems given by
\(f\cdot L=(f^{-1})^*L\).
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Set \(N=N(\mathbb S)\). By Theorem~\ref{thm:opper-geometrization},
\[
 N=U\rtimes T,
 \qquad
 U\simeq\mathbb G_a^{\phi_A(1,1)},
 \qquad
 T=H^1(S;\mathbf{k}^\times).
\]
Both \(U\) and \(T\) are divisible abelian groups.  For \(T\), this uses
the fact that \(\mathbf{k}\) is algebraically closed.  Since they generate \(N\),
we have \(N\subseteq\operatorname{DA}(D(\mathbb S))\).

Conversely, let \(A\le D(\mathbb S)\) be a divisible abelian subgroup.
Its image in \(\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\) is divisible and abelian. It is known that abelian subgroups of a
mapping class group are finitely generated \cite{BirmanLubotzkyMcCarthy}.
The same holds in the graded central extension because its kernel is
cyclic.  Thus the image of \(A\) is a
finitely generated divisible abelian group, hence trivial.  Therefore
\(
 A\subseteq N(\mathbb S)\). Therefore, every divisible abelian subgroup of \(D(\mathbb S)\) lies in \(N\),
and hence
\(
 \operatorname{DA}\bigl(D(\mathbb S)\bigr)\subseteq N(\mathbb S)\), which proves the first assertion.

Since \(N/U\simeq T\) is abelian, we have \([N,N]\subseteq U\) and
\(T\cap[N,N]=1\).  Consequently,
\[
 N^{\mathrm{ab}}\simeq U/[T,U]\times T.
\]
The subspace \([T,U]\) is the sum of the non-trivial weight spaces
for the torus action on \(U\). Hence \(U/[T,U]\) is a
\(\mathbf{k}\)-vector space with torsion-free additive group. Therefore
\[
 \operatorname{Tor}_{\ell^\infty}(N^{\mathrm{ab}})
 =T[\ell^\infty]
 =H^1(S;\mu_{\ell^\infty}).
\]
Inner automorphisms of \(N\) become trivial after abelianization.  The induced action
on the remaining torus is the local-system action described in \cite[Sections~7.1 and~10.1]{Opper2025}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:pruefer-rank-DPic}
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) be a graded surface with stops whose
partially wrapped Fukaya category is smooth and proper.
Suppose that \(S\) has genus \(g\geq2\) and \(b\) boundary
components. For every prime \(\ell\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pruefer-rank-DPic}
 \operatorname{prk}_\ell D(\mathbb S)=2g+b-1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Set \(D=D(\mathbb S)\) and \(N=N(\mathbb S)\). We first note that
\(D/N\simeq\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\) is virtually torsion free.
Indeed, the
ordinary mapping class group is virtually torsion free, and the kernel of
\eqref{eq:grading-extension} is infinite cyclic.  After replacing a
torsion-free finite-index subgroup downstairs by its full inverse image,
the same is true for the graded extension.

Let \(P\leq D\) be isomorphic to \((C_{\ell^\infty})^r\).  The image of
\(P\) in $D/N$ is torsion and divisible. Since \(D/N\) is virtually torsion
free, every torsion subgroup of \(D/N\) is finite. More precisely,  after passing to
a normal torsion-free subgroup of finite index, this image injects into the finite
quotient. Note that a finite divisible group is trivial. Therefore the image of \(P\) in \(D/N\) is trivial, so
\(P\le N\).

Write \(N=U\rtimes T\), where
\(U\simeq\mathbb G_a^{\phi_A(1,1)}\) and
\(T=H^1(S;\mathbf{k}^\times)\). Since \(U\) is torsion free,
\(P\cap U=1\), so projection to \(T\) is injective on
\(P\). In particular,
\[
 P[\ell]\simeq(\mathbb Z/\ell)^r
 \hookrightarrow T[\ell]
 \simeq(\mathbb Z/\ell)^{2g+b-1}.
\]
Thus \(r\leq2g+b-1\).
Conversely, the local-system torus
\(
 H^1(S;\mathbf{k}^\times)\simeq(\mathbf{k}^\times)^{2g+b-1}
\)
contains \((C_{\ell^\infty})^{2g+b-1}\), since \(\mathbf{k}\) is algebraically
closed of characteristic zero. Hence
\(\operatorname{prk}_\ell D\geq2g+b-1\). Together with the upper bound,
this proves \eqref{eq:pruefer-rank-DPic}.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:virtual-centre-DPic}
Under the assumptions of Lemma~\ref{lem:pruefer-rank-DPic}, put
\[
 \overline D(\mathbb S)
 =D(\mathbb S)/\operatorname{DA}\bigl(D(\mathbb S)\bigr).
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:virtual-centre-DPic}
 \operatorname{rvz}\overline D(\mathbb S)=b+1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:characteristic-geometrization-kernel} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:opper-geometrization}, we may identify
\(\overline D(\mathbb S)\) with \(\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\).  Let \(L\le\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\) be a
finite-index subgroup and let \(z\in Z(L)\). 
Abelian subgroups of \(H_\eta\) are finitely generated
\cite{BirmanLubotzkyMcCarthy}.  Since the kernel of the graded extension is
cyclic, the same holds for abelian subgroups of \(\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\).
In particular, \(Z(L)\) is finitely generated and has no non-trivial
divisible subgroup.

Let \(\bar z\in H_\eta\) denote the ungraded image of $z$. Every separating
essential curve \(c\subset S\) satisfies \(c^\vee=0\), so
\eqref{eq:twist-line-field} gives \(T_c\in H_\eta\).
Choose any graded
lift \(\widetilde T_c\) of \(T_c\). Since \(L\) has finite index in
\(\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\), there exists \(n(c)>0\) such that
\(\widetilde T_c^{\,n(c)}\in L\). Its ungraded image is
\(T_c^{n(c)}\). By centrality of \(z\), we obtain
\(
 T_{\bar z(c)}^{n(c)}=T_c^{n(c)}\).
Nonzero powers of twists determine their underlying curves, so
\(\bar z\) fixes every separating curve.

Lemma~\ref{lem:separating-fixer} then implies that the image of \(Z(L)\) in
\(H_\eta\) is contained in a finite extension of \(A_M\simeq\mathbb Z^b\). On the other hand, 
the kernel of \(\operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S)\to H_\eta\) is generated by the grading
shift. Hence,
\[
 \dim_{\mathbb Q}\bigl(Z(L)\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q\bigr)\leq b+1.
\]
This gives the upper bound for the reduced centre.

For the reverse inequality, let \(H_\eta^p\leq H_\eta\)
be the finite-index subgroup fixing every stop individually.
Choose a stop \(p\) and a lift of the tangent line
\(T_p\partial S\) to the grading cover. Each mapping class
in \(H_\eta^p\) has a unique graded lift fixing this chosen
point of the cover. This normalization is preserved under
composition and isotopy, and therefore splits the graded
extension over \(H_\eta^p\).

Boundary twists are central in \(H_\eta^p\) and generate
a free abelian subgroup of rank \(b\). In the normalized
splitting, their lifts, together with the deck translation
\([1]\), generate a central subgroup isomorphic to
\(\mathbb Z^{b+1}\). Since the centre is finitely generated, this
lattice survives in the reduced centre. This proves
\eqref{eq:virtual-centre-DPic}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Let
\[
 p=\operatorname{prk}_\ell\operatorname{DPic}(A),
 \qquad
 z=\operatorname{rvz}\!\left(
   \operatorname{DPic}(A)/\operatorname{DA}(\operatorname{DPic}(A))
 \right),
\]
where \(\ell\) is any prime.  Lemmas~\ref{lem:pruefer-rank-DPic} and
\ref{lem:virtual-centre-DPic} give
\begin{equation*}
 b=z-1,
 \qquad
 g=\frac{p-b+1}{2}=\frac{p-z+2}{2}.
\end{equation*}
The same formulas hold for \(A'\).  An abstract group isomorphism preserves
both \(p\) and \(z\), so \(g(S_A)=g(S_{A'})\) and
\(|\pi_0(\partial S_A)|=|\pi_0(\partial S_{A'})|\).  The classification of
compact connected oriented surfaces thus gives an orientation-preserving
diffeomorphism of the underlying surfaces.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:kida-rigidity}
Suppose that either \(g(S)\geq3\), or that \(g(S)=2\) and \(S\) has at
least two boundary components.  The natural homomorphism
\[
 \operatorname{Mod}^*(S)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}\bigl(\mathcal C_s(S)\bigr)
\]
is an isomorphism.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:stabilizer-determines-line-field}
Let \(S\) be a compact connected oriented surface of genus
at least two with non-empty boundary.
If two line fields \(\eta,\theta\) satisfy
\[
 \operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{PMod}(S)}(\eta)=\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{PMod}(S)}(\theta),
\]
then \(\eta\simeq\theta\).
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
We compare the two stabilizers using weighted products of Dehn
twists about genus-one bounding pairs, to which
\eqref{eq:weighted-bounding-pair} applies.

Let \(a,b\) be an oriented bounding pair which bounds a genus-one
subsurface, and set
\[
 A=\omega_\eta(a),\quad B=\omega_\eta(b),
 \qquad
 A'=\omega_\theta(a),\quad B'=\omega_\theta(b).
\]
By \eqref{eq:weighted-bounding-pair} and the assumed
equality of the two pure stabilizers, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \{(u,v)\in\mathbb Z^2:uA-vB=0\}
 =\{(u,v)\in\mathbb Z^2:uA'-vB'=0\}.
\end{equation*}
Hence the integer vectors \((A,B)\) and \((A',B')\) are proportional over \(\mathbb Q\).  Neither is zero, because
\eqref{eq:bounding-pair-winding-difference} gives
\[
 A-B=-4,
 \qquad A'-B'=-4.
\]
The proportionality factor is consequently one, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:winding-agrees-on-bp}
 \omega_\eta(a)=\omega_\theta(a),
 \qquad
 \omega_\eta(b)=\omega_\theta(b).
\end{equation}

Every nonseparating simple closed curve on a surface of genus at least two
belongs to a genus-one bounding pair.
Indeed, after cutting along the curve, the remaining surface
has genus at least one. One can choose a genus-one subsurface
with two boundary components adjacent to one of the new
boundary circles and disjoint from the original boundary.
Hence
\eqref{eq:winding-agrees-on-bp} holds for every nonseparating curve.  Write
\(\theta-\eta=\alpha\in H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\).  By
\eqref{eq:winding-difference}, \(\alpha\) vanishes on the homology class
of every nonseparating curve.  These classes span \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\).  Indeed,
a symplectic system gives the handle classes, while the class of each
boundary component is the difference between a nonseparating curve and
its band sum with that boundary component.  Thus \(\alpha=0\), and hence
\(\theta\simeq\eta\).
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Set
\[
 D=D(\mathbb S_A),\qquad D'=D(\mathbb S_{A'}),
 \qquad N=N(\mathbb S_A),\qquad N'=N(\mathbb S_{A'}).
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:characteristic-geometrization-kernel}, we have
\(\Phi(N)=N'\).  Thus \(\Phi\) induces an isomorphism of graded mapping
class groups.  Since \(\Phi([1])=[1]\), quotienting by the central grading
shift in \eqref{eq:grading-extension} gives an isomorphism
\(
 \phi:H_{\eta_A}\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\eta_{A'}}\). Part~\textup{(i)} implies that \(S_A\) and \(S_{A'}\) have the same genus
and the same number of boundary components. We may therefore
choose an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism between them and
temporarily identify both with a fixed surface \(S=\Sigma_{g,b}\).
Denote by \(M,M'\) and \(\eta,\eta'\) the stop sets and line fields
transported to \(S\). 

Suppose first that \(S=\Sigma_{2,1}\).  By
\eqref{eq:ell-local-systems}, the characteristic group
\(
 V(\mathbb S)=N(\mathbb S)^{\mathrm{ab}}[2]
\)
is naturally \(H^1(S;\mathbb F_2)\).  Conjugation on it is the mod-two
homological representation of \(H_\eta\). After the stops are forgotten,
\(H_\eta\) maps onto \(\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S)}(\eta)\), while its
boundary-rotation kernel acts trivially on cohomology. Its image is
therefore the group \(R_\eta\) of
Lemma~\ref{lem:S21-two-torsion}. The isomorphism \(\Phi\)
conjugates \(R_{\eta_A}\) to \(R_{\eta_{A'}}\), so the two
images have the same order.  Lemma~\ref{lem:S21-two-torsion} shows that the
two line fields have the same \(\sigma\)-invariant and, when defined, the
same Arf invariant.  Their boundary winding numbers are both \(-6\).
Theorem~\ref{thm:line-field-classification} now gives an
orientation-preserving diffeomorphism \(f:S_A\to S_{A'}\) with
\(f^*\eta_{A'}\simeq\eta_A\).  This proves the exceptional case.

We henceforth assume that either \(g\geq3\), or \(g=2\) and \(b\geq2\). Fix a prime \(\ell\), and set
\(
 L_\ell(\mathbb S)
 =\operatorname{Tor}_{\ell^\infty}
 \bigl(N(\mathbb S)^{\mathrm{ab}}\bigr)\).
Both \(N\) and \(L_\ell(\mathbb S)\) are characteristic in the data under
consideration.  Hence \(\phi\) identifies the kernels of the two
conjugation representations on \(L_\ell\).  By
\eqref{eq:ell-local-systems}, a mapping class acts trivially on
\(L_\ell\) precisely when it acts trivially on \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\).  An integral
matrix that is the identity on all \(\ell\)-power characters is congruent
to the identity modulo \(\ell^n\) for every \(n\), and hence is the
identity.  Thus \(\phi\) restricts to an isomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:framed-torelli-isomorphism}
 J_\eta\xrightarrow{\sim}J_{\eta'},
 \qquad
 J_\eta
 =H_\eta\cap
 \ker\bigl(\operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\to\operatorname{Aut}H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\bigr).
\end{equation}

We next forget the stops in \eqref{eq:framed-torelli-isomorphism}.
Every element of \(J_\eta\) preserves each boundary component.
For \(b\geq2\), the subgroup of \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\)
generated by the oriented boundary classes is
\(\mathbb Z^b/\mathbb Z(1,\ldots,1)\), and the natural
permutation action on this lattice is faithful. Trivial action
on absolute homology excludes a non-trivial boundary
permutation. For \(b=1\), the assertion is automatic.  Recall that isotopies in \(\operatorname{Mod}^*(S)\) may move
points of \(\partial S\), and let
\(\operatorname{PMod}(S)\leq\operatorname{Mod}(S)\) be the subgroup preserving every boundary
component setwise.  The forgetful homomorphism
\[
 q_M:\operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}(S)\subset\operatorname{Mod}^*(S)
\]
forgets the stops.  Denote by \(\mathcal I(S)\) the Torelli group and by
\(\mathcal K(S)\) the Johnson kernel, generated by twists about separating
curves, in the conventions of \cite{Kida}.  We distinguish these from the
absolute homological kernel
\[
 \mathcal I_0(S)
 =\ker\bigl(\operatorname{PMod}(S)\to\operatorname{Aut}H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\bigr).
\]
If \(\theta-\eta=\alpha\in H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\) and
\(x\in\mathcal I_0(S)\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:torelli-displacement-independence}
 x^*\theta-\theta
 =(x^*\eta-\eta)+(x^*\alpha-\alpha)
 =x^*\eta-\eta.
\end{equation}
Every element of \(\mathcal I_0(S)\) fixes each boundary component and has a
representative preserving the finite stop set after an isotopy in boundary
collars.  Moreover, the kernel of \(q_M\)
preserves every line field.  Hence \(q_M(J_\eta)\) is exactly the subgroup
of \(\mathcal I_0(S)\) preserving \(\eta\).  Equation~\eqref{eq:torelli-displacement-independence} therefore shows that the
subgroup
\(
 \mathcal C_0(S)\coloneqq q_M(J_\eta)
\)
is independent of \(\eta\).  It is the kernel of the Chillingworth class
restricted to the absolute homological kernel \cite{Chillingworth}.
Separating twists preserve every line field, and the capping argument in
\cite[Section~2]{Kida} gives
\(\mathcal I_0(S)\subseteq\mathcal I(S)\). Consequently
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal K(S)\subseteq\mathcal C_0(S)\subseteq\mathcal I(S).
\end{equation*}

The kernel of \(q_M:J_\eta\to\mathcal C_0(S)\) consists of boundary
rotations and is central in \(J_\eta\).  Conversely, if
\(z\in Z(J_\eta)\), then \(q_M(z)\) centralizes \(\mathcal C_0(S)\), hence
centralizes every separating twist.  Conjugating a twist shows that it
fixes every vertex of the separating-curve complex.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:kida-rigidity}, the action on that complex is faithful in
the present range.  Hence \(q_M(z)=1\).  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:center-pure-torelli}
 Z(J_\eta)=\ker(q_M|_{J_\eta}),
 \qquad J_\eta/Z(J_\eta)\simeq\mathcal C_0(S).
\end{equation}
The same statement holds for \(J_{\eta'}\).  Since \(\phi\) preserves
centres, it induces an isomorphism
\(
 \overline\phi:\mathcal C_0(S)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal C_0(S)\).

Lemma~\ref{lem:twists-in-chillingworth-kernel} shows that
\(\overline\phi\) permutes the cyclic axes of separating twists.  Distinct
such axes commute exactly when their curves are disjoint, so
\(\overline\phi\) induces an automorphism of the separating-curve complex.
By Theorem~\ref{thm:kida-rigidity}, there is an element
\(f\in\operatorname{Mod}^*(S)\) inducing this automorphism. To show that
\(\overline\phi\) is conjugation by \(f\), take
\(x\in\mathcal C_0(S)\), a separating curve \(c\), and an integer
\(n\neq0\). In the identity
\[
 \overline\phi(xT_c^nx^{-1})
 =\overline\phi(x)\,\overline\phi(T_c^n)\,
  \overline\phi(x)^{-1},
\]
the left-hand side lies on the twist axis associated with
\(f(x(c))\), while the right-hand side lies on the twist axis
associated with \(\overline\phi(x)(f(c))\). Hence
\[
 f(x(c))=\overline\phi(x)(f(c))
\]
for every separating curve \(c\).  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:kida-rigidity}, the action of \(\operatorname{Mod}^*(S)\) on the
separating-curve complex is faithful in the present range.  It follows that
\(\overline\phi(x)=fxf^{-1}\) for all \(x\in\mathcal C_0(S)\).  Returning to
the central extensions \eqref{eq:center-pure-torelli}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kida-geometricity-on-J}
 q_{M'}\bigl(\phi(x)\bigr)=f q_M(x)f^{-1}
 \qquad\text{ for }x\in J_\eta.
\end{equation}
Undoing the preliminary identification of the two surfaces, \(f\) is a
diffeomorphism \(S_A\to S_{A'}\), not yet known to preserve orientation.

Equation~\eqref{eq:kida-geometricity-on-J} extends from \(J_\eta\) to the
whole line-field stabilizer.  Indeed, take \(y\in H_\eta\).  Since
\(J_\eta\triangleleft H_\eta\), comparison of
\eqref{eq:kida-geometricity-on-J} for \(x\) and \(yxy^{-1}\) shows that
\[
 q_{M'}\bigl(\phi(y)\bigr)^{-1}f q_M(y)f^{-1}
\]
 centralizes \(\mathcal C_0(S)\).  It is trivial because an element
centralizing \(\mathcal C_0(S)\) fixes every separating curve, and the same
faithfulness statement applies.  Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometricity-on-stabilizer}
 q_{M'}\bigl(\phi(y)\bigr)=f q_M(y)f^{-1}
 \qquad\text{ for }y\in H_\eta.
\end{equation}

For a line field \(\lambda\) and any finite stop set \(P\) meeting every
boundary component, put
\(H_{\lambda,P}=\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S,P)}(\lambda)\).  Then
\[
 q_P(H_{\lambda,P})\cap\operatorname{PMod}(S)=\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{PMod}(S)}(\lambda).
\]
Indeed, every class in \(\operatorname{PMod}(S)\) has a representative which is the
identity on boundary collars and therefore preserves \(P\).  The kernel of
\(q_P\) preserves every line field.  Thus the displayed group is independent
of the chosen stop set.

Since \(\phi\) is surjective, equation
\eqref{eq:geometricity-on-stabilizer} gives
\[
 q_{M'}(H_{\eta'})=f q_M(H_\eta)f^{-1}.
\]
Intersecting with the pure mapping class groups of the surfaces without stops and putting
\(\theta=f^*\eta'\), we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:equal-pure-coarse-stabilizers}
 \operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{PMod}(S)}(\eta)
 =\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{PMod}(S)}(\theta).
\end{equation}

Lemma~\ref{lem:stabilizer-determines-line-field}, applied to
\eqref{eq:equal-pure-coarse-stabilizers}, gives
\(f^*\eta_{A'}\simeq\eta_A\).
It remains only to correct the orientation of \(f\).  If \(f\) preserves
orientation, there is nothing to prove.  Otherwise,
Lemma~\ref{lem:orientation-correction} gives an orientation-reversing
diffeomorphism \(\rho:S_A\to S_A\) satisfying
\(\rho^*\eta_A\simeq\eta_A\).  Replacing \(f\) by \(f\circ\rho\), we obtain
an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism and
\[
 (f\circ\rho)^*\eta_{A'}
 =\rho^*f^*\eta_{A'}
 \simeq\rho^*\eta_A
 \simeq\eta_A.
\]
This proves the theorem.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:geometric-reduction}
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) and
\(\mathbb S'=(S',M',\eta')\) satisfy the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:derived-reconstruction}, and suppose that both
surfaces have at least two boundary components. A shift-preserving
isomorphism
\[
 \Phi:D(\mathbb S)\xrightarrow{\sim}D(\mathbb S')
\]
identifies the geometrisation kernels and induces an isomorphism
\(\phi:H_\eta\to H_{\eta'}\). It also identifies
\[
 J_\eta=H_\eta\cap
 \ker\bigl(\operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\to\operatorname{Aut}H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\bigr)
\]
with \(J_{\eta'}\), and carries \(C=Z(J_\eta)\) onto
\(C'=Z(J_{\eta'})\). There is a diffeomorphism
\(f:S\to S'\), possibly orientation reversing, such that, for every $y\in H_\eta$, we have
\[
 f^*\eta'\simeq\eta,
 \qquad
 q_{M'}\bigl(\phi(y)\bigr)=f q_M(y)f^{-1},
\]
where \(q_M\) and \(q_{M'}\) forget the stops.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:characteristic-geometrization-kernel}, the
geometrisation kernels are identified, and quotienting by the shift gives \(\phi\).
The conjugation action on the torsion subgroup of the abelianized kernel
yields an identification \(J_\eta\simeq J_{\eta'}\). The centre calculation
\eqref{eq:center-pure-torelli} then identifies \(C\) with \(C'\).
The rigidity argument leading to
\eqref{eq:geometricity-on-stabilizer} produces the diffeomorphism \(f\) and the displayed
conjugation formula. Finally,
Lemma~\ref{lem:stabilizer-determines-line-field} gives
\(f^*\eta'\simeq\eta\).
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:countable-fibres}
For every integer \(g\geq2\), there is a family
\(\{A_k\}_{k\in\mathbb Z_{\geq1}}\) of connected graded gentle algebras which are
finite-dimensional, homologically smooth, and proper. After the stops are
forgotten, their surface models have the same underlying graded surface
\((\Sigma_{g,3},\eta)\). Their stop distributions are
\[
 \bigl(k(2g-1),k(2g-1)+1,1\bigr).
\]
They satisfy
\[
 \mathfrak D(A_k)\simeq\mathfrak D(A_l)
 \qquad\text{for all }k,l\geq1,
\]
whereas the categories \(\operatorname{per}(A_k)\) are pairwise non-equivalent. The
line field \(\eta\) is induced by a vector framing, and the fibre containing
this family is countably infinite.

Moreover, for every \(R\geq1\), there is a fibre of \(\mathfrak D\)
containing more than \(R\) pairwise non-equivalent categories of genus
\(g\) with the same rank of the Grothendieck group.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:common-pointed-group-family}
For every \(r,s\geq1\), the graded surface \(\mathbb S_{r,s}\)
admits a connected finite-dimensional, homologically smooth and
proper graded gentle algebra model \(A_{r,s}\).
Put \(N=H^1(S;\mathbf{k}^\times)\), with the natural action of \(F_0\)
induced by mapping classes.
There are shift-preserving identifications
\[
 \operatorname{DPic}(A_{r,s})\simeq
 G=(N\rtimes F_0)\times
   \mathbb Z\langle\rho_1\rangle\times
   \mathbb Z\langle\rho_2\rangle\times
   \mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle
\]
for all \(r,s\geq1\), where the two rotation factors are identified
with fixed copies of \(\mathbb Z\) as \(r,s\) vary.
We use the same symbols for \(a,\rho_1,\rho_2\) and their
corresponding elements in \(G\).
Let \(\mathsf S_{r,s}\in\operatorname{DPic}(A_{r,s})\) be the element represented
by the Serre functor of \(\operatorname{per}(A_{r,s})\). Under these identifications,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:normalized-serre-family}
 \mathsf S_{r,s}
 =a^{-1}\rho_1^{-(qr+1)}\rho_2^{qs-1}[1]^{e_g},
\end{equation}
where \(e_g\in\mathbb Z\) is independent of \(r,s\).
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Put
\[
 H_{r,s}=\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S,M_{r,s})}(\eta).
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:framed-stabilizer-presentation}, whose subgroup
\(F_0\) is the relative framing stabilizer defined above, gives
the decompositions
\eqref{eq:F0-splitting}--\eqref{eq:Hrs-splitting}.

There is also a canonical splitting of the grading extension for this
family.  Let \(p\) be the unique stop on \(B_3\) and choose a lift of the
tangent line \(T_pB_3\) to the grading cover.  Every element of
\(H_{r,s}\) fixes this tangent line and has a unique graded lift fixing the
chosen point above it.  The product of two normalized lifts is again
normalized, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:graded-extension-splitting-family}
 \operatorname{MCG}_{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb S_{r,s})
 \simeq H_{r,s}\times\mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle.
\end{equation}


Choose a minimal full formal system of graded arcs and let \(A_{r,s}\)
be the basic total graded endomorphism algebra of their direct sum.  The existence and the smoothness and
properness criteria are given in \cite[Proposition~2.5(1),(3),(4)]{Opper2025}.
The ribbon graph dual to the chosen full system of arcs is connected because
\(S\) is connected and the complementary regions are discs.  Equivalently,
the underlying graph of the gentle quiver of \(A_{r,s}\) is connected.
Hence \(A_{r,s}\) is a connected, finite-dimensional, homologically smooth,
and proper graded gentle algebra and
\[
 \operatorname{per}(A_{r,s})\simeq\mathcal W(\mathbb S_{r,s}).
\]

Put \(N=H^1(S;\mathbf{k}^\times)\).  Since all three windings in
\eqref{eq:chosen-line-windings} are non-zero, the additive factor in
Theorem~\ref{thm:opper-geometrization} is absent. Thus
\(N=N(\mathbb S_{r,s})\) for every \(r,s\). Equations~\eqref{eq:derived-picard-formula},
\eqref{eq:Hrs-splitting}, and
\eqref{eq:graded-extension-splitting-family} identify every derived
Picard group with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:common-derived-picard-group}
 G=(N\rtimes F_0)\times\mathbb Z^2\times\mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle.
\end{equation}
Only \(F_0\) acts non-trivially on \(N\).  The collar rotations and the
shift act trivially and the former are isotopic to the identity after the
stops are forgotten.  Moreover, \(a=T_3T_1^{-q}T_2^q\) is a product of
boundary twists and acts trivially on \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\), hence on \(N\).
Since \(a\in Z(F_0)\), it is central in \(N\rtimes F_0\).  In particular,
the group in
\eqref{eq:common-derived-picard-group}, with its distinguished shift, is
independent of \((r,s)\).

It remains to identify the actual Serre element, since geometrisation
alone leaves a possible local-system factor and a shift. The argument
in Lemma~\ref{lem:framed-family-serre-normalization} tests that factor
on rank-one band objects supported on zero-winding curves. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:gradable-curves-span}, their classes generate
\(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\), so triviality of these holonomies eliminates the
entire local-system factor. The same band calculation determines the
shift exponent from the normalized action over the fixed core. Hence
it is independent of \(r,s\). This gives
\eqref{eq:normalized-serre-family} and completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:primitive-serre-vectors}
If \(v_{r,s}\) and \(v_{r',s'}\) are primitive, then
\[
 \mathfrak D(\mathbb S_{r,s})\simeq
 \mathfrak D(\mathbb S_{r',s'}).
\]
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Use the common presentation in
Lemma~\ref{lem:common-pointed-group-family}. For a primitive vector
\(v=(a,b)^T\), choose integers \(u,w\) with \(ua+wb=1\), and put
\[
 B(v)=\begin{pmatrix}u&w\\-b&a\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathbb Z).
\]
Then \(B(v)v=(1,0)^T\). Consequently the explicit integral matrix
\[
 P=B(v_{r',s'})^{-1}B(v_{r,s})
\]
satisfies \(Pv_{r,s}=v_{r',s'}\), and hence
\(P(-v_{r,s})=-v_{r',s'}\). The automorphism of
\[
 (N\rtimes F_0)\times\mathbb Z^2\times\mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle
\]
which is the identity on \(N\rtimes F_0\) and on the shift factor,
and is given by \(P\) on \(\mathbb Z^2\), preserves \([1]\). Formula~\eqref{eq:normalized-serre-family} shows that it sends
\(\mathsf S_{r,s}\) to \(\mathsf S_{r',s'}\).
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Fix \(g\geq2\), put \(q=2g+1\), and use the line field and algebras
from Lemma~\ref{lem:common-pointed-group-family}. Set
\[
 d=q-2=2g-1,\qquad m_k=kd\quad\text{ for } k\geq1,
\]
and take \((r,s)=(m_k,m_k+1)\). Put
\(A_k=A_{m_k,m_k+1}\). Then
\[
 \gcd\bigl(qm_k+1,q(m_k+1)-1\bigr)
 =\gcd(qm_k+1,q-2)=1.
\]
Indeed, \(d\mid m_k\), and hence \(qm_k+1\equiv1\pmod d\).
Thus every vector \(v_{m_k,m_k+1}\) is primitive.
Lemma~\ref{lem:primitive-serre-vectors} identifies all the triples
\(\mathfrak D(\mathbb S_{m_k,m_k+1})\).

The total number of stops is \(2m_k+2\), so these marked surfaces
are pairwise non-isomorphic. Theorem~\ref{thm:complete-surface-invariant}
therefore gives
\[
 \operatorname{per}(A_{m_k,m_k+1})\not\simeq
 \operatorname{per}(A_{m_l,m_l+1})
 \qquad\text{for }k\neq l.
\]
The fibre is countably infinite. It contains the displayed infinite
family, while the set of isomorphism classes of graded surfaces with
stops is countable. To see the latter assertion, note that there are
countably many topological types of compact marked surfaces. On a fixed
surface the homotopy classes of line fields form a torsor over the
finitely generated group \(H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\).

For the fixed-rank assertion, let \(R\geq1\) and choose a prime
\(p>R+2\) which does not divide \(q\). Consider
\[
 (r,s,1)=(r,p-r,1),
 \qquad 1\leq r\leq p-1,
\]
and omit the unique residue satisfying \(qr+1\equiv0\pmod p\).
There remain \(p-2>R\) values. For each of them,
\[
 \gcd(qr+1,q(p-r)-1)
 =\gcd(qr+1,qp)=1.
\]
The corresponding vectors \(v_{r,p-r}\) are primitive, so all these
surfaces lie in one fibre of \(\mathfrak D\). Their total number of stops is
\(p+1\), and \eqref{eq:charge-rank} gives the common rank
\[
 \operatorname{rk} K(\mathbb S_{r,p-r})=2g+p+2.
\]
The boundary winding numbers \(2,-2,-2q\) are pairwise distinct.
Every graded diffeomorphism therefore preserves each boundary
component. It follows that two of these stopped graded surfaces are
isomorphic only when their values of \(r\) agree. The complete surface
invariant now shows that the corresponding categories are pairwise
non-equivalent.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
For \(g=2\), the first three stop distributions in the infinite family
are \((3,4,1)\), \((6,7,1)\), and \((9,10,1)\). Their Serre elements
have rotation coordinates \((-16,19)\), \((-31,34)\), and
\((-46,49)\), respectively. Each is primitive, so
Lemma~\ref{lem:primitive-serre-vectors} identifies the three shift-
and Serre-pointed derived Picard groups. Their Grothendieck ranks
are \(13\), \(19\), and \(25\), and hence the categories are pairwise
non-equivalent.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
\label{ex:low-genus-failure}
For genus zero, consider a pair of pants with one stop on each boundary
component. Choose line fields \(\eta\) and \(\eta'\) with respective
boundary winding numbers \((2,4,-8)\) and \((2,6,-10)\). These two
triples of boundary winding numbers satisfy
\eqref{eq:line-field-boundary-coherence}, and hence are realized by line
fields. In each triple, the three entries are non-zero and pairwise
distinct. The corresponding line-field stabilizer therefore fixes every
boundary component. The pure mapping class group of a pair of pants with
one stop on each boundary component is freely generated by the three
boundary twists. Consequently,
\[
 H_\eta\simeq H_{\eta'}\simeq\mathbb Z^3.
\]
Choose one of the three stops and a lift of its tangent line to the grading
cover. The unique graded lift fixing this point splits the grading extension
\eqref{eq:grading-extension}. Since boundary twists act trivially on
absolute homology, Theorem~\ref{thm:opper-geometrization} gives, for both
line fields,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-zero-common-group}
 D(\mathbb S)\simeq
 H^1(\Sigma_{0,3};\mathbf{k}^\times)\times\mathbb Z^3
 \times\mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle
 \simeq(\mathbf{k}^\times)^2\times\mathbb Z^4.
\end{equation}
Let \(R_\partial=T_1T_2T_3\). The underlying ungraded mapping class
of the Serre functor is \(R_\partial^{-1}\). Thus, in the coordinates
of \eqref{eq:genus-zero-common-group}, the two Serre elements have
the form
\[
 \mathsf S=nR_\partial^{-1}[1]^e,
 \qquad
 \mathsf S'=n'R_\partial^{-1}[1]^{e'},
\]
where \(n,n'\in(\mathbf{k}^\times)^2\) and \(e,e'\in\mathbb Z\). The vector
\((-1,-1,-1)\) is primitive in \(\mathbb Z^3\).
Choose homomorphisms \(\mu,\lambda\colon\mathbb Z^3\to\mathbb Z\) such that
\[
 \mu(R_\partial^{-1})=1,
 \qquad
 \lambda(R_\partial^{-1})=e'-e.
\] 
Writing \(c=n'n^{-1}\), the automorphism
\[
 (u,x,j)\longmapsto
 \bigl(u c^{\mu(x)},x,j+\lambda(x)\bigr)
\]
fixes \([1]\) and sends \(\mathsf S\) to \(\mathsf S'\). The two
shift- and Serre-pointed derived Picard groups are therefore isomorphic.

For genus one, realize \(\Sigma_{1,1}\) by removing an
open disc from the flat torus \(\mathbb R^2/\mathbb Z^2\),
and place one stop on its boundary. Restrict a constant
nowhere-vanishing vector field \(\xi_0\) from the torus
and put \(\eta_0=\mathbb R\xi_0\). Standard horizontal
and vertical nonseparating curves have winding number zero.
Their Dehn twists preserve \(\eta_0\) by
\eqref{eq:twist-line-field} and these twists generate the
mapping class group of the one-holed torus. Thus \(\eta_0\)
is invariant under this group. Since the group acts
transitively on nonseparating curves, every such curve has
zero winding. Boundary coherence gives
\(\omega_{\eta_0}(\partial\Sigma_{1,1})=-2\).

For a primitive class
\(\alpha\in H^1(\Sigma_{1,1};\mathbb Z)\), set
\(
 \eta_1=\eta_0+\alpha\), and
 \(\eta_2=\eta_0+2\alpha\).  It follows that, for
\(d\geq1\),
\[
 f^*(\eta_0+d\alpha)\simeq\eta_0+d\alpha
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 f^*\alpha=\alpha.
\]
Since \(H^1(\Sigma_{1,1};\mathbb Z)\) is torsion free, the stabilizers of
\(\eta_1\) and \(\eta_2\) coincide. Denote their common value by
\(H_\alpha\).

The stabilizer of a primitive class in \(\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)\) is
infinite cyclic. Its inverse image in the mapping class group of the
one-holed torus fits into a central extension
\[
 1\longrightarrow\mathbb Z\langle T_{\partial}\rangle
 \longrightarrow H_\alpha
 \longrightarrow\mathbb Z
 \longrightarrow1.
\]
This extension splits because \(H^2(\mathbb Z;\mathbb Z)=0\). Choosing a lift \(U\) of
the positive generator gives
\[
 H_\alpha\simeq
 \mathbb Z\langle U\rangle\times\mathbb Z\langle T_{\partial}\rangle.
\]
In particular, \(T_{\partial}\) is primitive in \(H_\alpha\). Normalizing
graded lifts at the unique stop again splits
\eqref{eq:grading-extension}. The two derived Picard groups are therefore
identified with the same semidirect product
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-one-common-group}
 \bigl(H^1(\Sigma_{1,1};\mathbf{k}^\times)\rtimes H_\alpha\bigr)
 \times\mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle.
\end{equation}
The underlying ungraded mapping class of each Serre functor is
\(T_\partial^{-1}\). Hence their Serre elements have the form
\[
 \mathsf S_d=n_dT_\partial^{-1}[1]^{e_d},
 \qquad d=1,2,
\]
where
\[
 n_d\in H^1(\Sigma_{1,1};\mathbf{k}^\times)^{H_\alpha}.
\]
The invariance of \(n_d\) follows from the centrality of the Serre functor.
Since \(T_\partial^{-1}\) is primitive, the same shear construction
as in genus zero gives an automorphism of
\eqref{eq:genus-one-common-group} that fixes \([1]\) and sends
\(\mathsf S_1\) to \(\mathsf S_2\). Hence the two shift- and
Serre-pointed derived Picard groups are isomorphic.

The corresponding underlying graded surfaces are nevertheless different. In
genus zero, their unordered collections of boundary winding numbers are
distinct. In genus one, \eqref{eq:genus-one-line-field-invariant} gives
\(
 \widetilde{\mathcal A}(\eta_1)=1,
 \widetilde{\mathcal A}(\eta_2)=2
\). Theorem~\ref{thm:line-field-classification} shows that neither pair of
underlying graded surfaces is isomorphic. A minimal full formal system of graded arcs on each
surface produces a connected graded gentle algebra that is
finite-dimensional, homologically smooth, and proper, as in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:common-pointed-group-family}.
Theorem~\ref{thm:complete-surface-invariant} also shows that the two categories in
each pair are non-equivalent.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:serre-reconstruction}
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) and
\(\mathbb S'=(S',M',\eta')\) be compact graded surfaces with stops, with at
least one stop on every boundary component, and suppose that their
partially wrapped Fukaya categories are smooth and proper.  Assume that
\(\eta=\mathbb R\xi\) and \(\eta'=\mathbb R\xi'\) are induced by
nowhere-vanishing vector fields.  If a \(\mathbb Q\)-linear isomorphism
\[
 K(\mathbb S)\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q
 \xrightarrow{\sim}
 K(\mathbb S')\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q
\]
intertwines the actions of the two Serre functors, then there is an
orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of surfaces with stops
\[
 (S,M)\simeq(S',M').
\]
In particular, the conjugacy class over \(\mathbb Q\) of the Serre action determines the
genus, the number of boundary components, and the unordered collection of
numbers of stops on those components, in every genus.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:serre-recovers-boundary-number}
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) satisfy the assumptions of
Theorem~\ref{thm:serre-reconstruction}, put
\(K=K(\mathbb S)\), and set
\[
 \mathsf C=(\mathsf S[-1])_*\in\operatorname{Aut}(K).
\]
The automorphism \(\mathsf C\) has a unipotent positive power.
Let \(n_{\mathsf C}\) be the least positive integer such that
\(\mathsf C^{n_{\mathsf C}}\) is unipotent over \(\mathbb Q\).
Then the number \(b\) of boundary components is
\[
 b=1+\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb Q}
 \bigl(\mathsf C^{n_{\mathsf C}}-I\bigr).
\]
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Write
\[
 \partial S=B_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_b,
 \qquad m_i=|M\cap B_i|.
\]
Since \(\eta=\mathbb R\xi\),
Theorem~\ref{thm:charge-lattice} gives
\[
 K\simeq H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Z).
\]
Let \(\mathsf S\) be the Serre functor and set
\[
 \mathsf C=(\mathsf S[-1])_*=-\mathsf S_*
 \in\operatorname{Aut}(K).
\]
By \eqref{eq:serre-rotation-action}, there is a sign
\(\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reduced-serre-versus-rotation}
 \mathsf C=\varepsilon R_*^{-1},
\end{equation}
where \(R\) is simultaneous positive one-step boundary rotation.
We first show that \(\mathsf C\) has a unipotent power. Set
\(L=\operatorname{lcm}(2,m_1,\ldots,m_b)\). Then
\(\varepsilon^L=1\), and \(\mathsf C^L\)
is induced by a product of powers of boundary twists. For
each boundary twist, its difference from the identity has
image in the span of the absolute boundary classes, on which
all boundary twists act trivially. Consequently
\((\mathsf C^L-I)^2=0\), so \(\mathsf C^L\) is unipotent.
We may therefore define
\begin{equation*}
 n_{\mathsf C}=
 \min\{n\geq1:\mathsf C^n
 \text{ is unipotent over }\mathbb Q\}.
\end{equation*}
We claim that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary-rank-from-serre}
 b=1+\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb Q}
 \bigl(\mathsf C^{n_{\mathsf C}}-I\bigr).
\end{equation}

Suppose first that \(S\) is not a disc.  Then
\(H_1(S;\mathbb Q)\neq0\), and \(R\) acts trivially on absolute homology.
Thus \(\mathsf C\) acts there by the scalar \(\varepsilon\), and
unipotence of \(\mathsf C^{n_{\mathsf C}}\) implies
\(\varepsilon^{n_{\mathsf C}}=1\). On
\(\widetilde H_0(M^\circ;\mathbb Q)\), the rotation is a product of cycles of
lengths \(m_1,\ldots,m_b\).  Every non-trivial eigenvalue of each cycle
survives in reduced homology, so \(m_i\mid n_{\mathsf C}\) for every \(i\). It
follows that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-as-boundary-twists}
 \mathsf C^{n_{\mathsf C}}=R_*^{-n_{\mathsf C}}
 =\prod_{i=1}^b(T_i)_*^{-n_{\mathsf C}/m_i},
\end{equation}
where \(T_i\) is the positive boundary twist about \(B_i\).

Orient each \(B_i\) by the boundary orientation and put
\(c_i=[B_i]\in H_1(S;\mathbb Q)\).  These classes span a vector space of
dimension \(b-1\), with the single relation
\[
 c_1+\cdots+c_b=0.
\]
For \(x\in H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Q)\), write
\[
 \partial x=\sum_{p\in M^\circ}a_p[p],
 \qquad
 \lambda_i(x)=\sum_{p\in M^\circ\cap B_i}a_p.
\]
Then \(\sum_i\lambda_i(x)=0\).  Put
\[
 W_\partial=
 \{(u_1,\ldots,u_b)\in\mathbb Q^b:\textstyle\sum_i u_i=0\}.
\]
Moreover, the map
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary-charge-map}
 H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Q)\longrightarrow W_\partial,
 \qquad x\longmapsto(\lambda_1(x),\ldots,\lambda_b(x))
\end{equation}
is surjective since relative arcs between points of \(M^\circ\) on distinct boundary
components realize the vectors \(e_i-e_j\).

With the boundary orientation fixed above, a collar representative
of \(T_i\) adds \(c_i\) to an oriented relative arc ending on \(B_i\)
and \(-c_i\) to one starting there. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary-twist-relative-homology}
 (T_i)_*x=x+\lambda_i(x)c_i.
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:power-as-boundary-twists} and
\eqref{eq:boundary-twist-relative-homology}, we obtain
\[
 \bigl(\mathsf C^{n_{\mathsf C}}-I\bigr)x
 =-\sum_{i=1}^b
 \frac{n_{\mathsf C}}{m_i}\lambda_i(x)c_i.
\]
Identify the span of the \(c_i\) with
\(\mathbb Q^b/\mathbb Q(1,\ldots,1)\). Since multiplication by
\(-1\) does not change the rank, it suffices to consider the map
\begin{equation*}
 W_\partial\longrightarrow\mathbb Q^b/\mathbb Q(1,\ldots,1),
 \qquad
 (u_i)\longmapsto
 \left(\frac{n_{\mathsf C}}{m_i}u_i\right).
\end{equation*}
This map is injective.  Indeed, if its value is zero, then
\((n_{\mathsf C}/m_i)u_i=c\) for one constant \(c\). Hence
\[
 0=\sum_i u_i=\frac{c}{n_{\mathsf C}}\sum_i m_i,
\]
which forces \(c=0\), and then \(u_i=0\) for every \(i\).  By the
surjectivity of \eqref{eq:boundary-charge-map}, the image of
\(\mathsf C^{n_{\mathsf C}}-I\) therefore has dimension \(b-1\),
proving \eqref{eq:boundary-rank-from-serre} when \(S\) is not a disc.

If \(S\) is a disc, then \(b=1\) and
\(H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Q)=\widetilde H_0(M^\circ;\mathbb Q)\). The
operator \(\mathsf C\) is semisimple of finite order. Its least unipotent
power is therefore the identity, so
\(\operatorname{rk}(\mathsf C^{n_{\mathsf C}}-I)=0=b-1\). Thus
\eqref{eq:boundary-rank-from-serre} holds in every case.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:serre-recovers-stops}
Under the assumptions of
Lemma~\ref{lem:serre-recovers-boundary-number}, the rational conjugacy class
of \(\mathsf C=(\mathsf S[-1])_*\) determines the unordered stop distribution
\(\{m_1,\ldots,m_b\}\).
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Write
\[
 \partial S=B_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_b,
 \qquad m_i=|M\cap B_i|,
\]
and put \(K=K(\mathbb S)\). By
Theorem~\ref{thm:charge-lattice}, we identify
\(K\simeq H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Z)\). By
\eqref{eq:serre-rotation-action}, there is a sign
\(\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}\) such that
\(\mathsf C=\varepsilon R_*^{-1}\), where \(R\) is simultaneous
positive one-step boundary rotation. Consider the exact sequence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:relative-homology-sequence}
 0\longrightarrow H_1(S;\mathbb Z)
 \longrightarrow H_1(S,M^\circ;\mathbb Z)
 \longrightarrow\widetilde H_0(M^\circ;\mathbb Z)
 \longrightarrow0,
\end{equation}
which is equivariant for \(R\). The rotation acts trivially on
\(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\). On \(H_0(M^\circ;\mathbb Z)\), it is a product of disjoint
cycles of lengths \(m_1,\ldots,m_b\). Since
\(\operatorname{rk} H_1(S;\mathbb Z)=2g+b-1\), multiplicativity of characteristic
polynomials in \eqref{eq:relative-homology-sequence} gives, when
\(S\) is not a disc,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rotation-polynomial}
 \det(tI-R_*)=(t-1)^{2g+b-2}\prod_{i=1}^b(t^{m_i}-1).
\end{equation}
For a disc, the right-hand side is understood as
\((t^{m_1}-1)/(t-1)\). The inverse rotation has the same cycle
lengths and is also the identity on absolute homology, so
\(R_*^{-1}\) has the same characteristic polynomial as \(R_*\).

For ordinary gentle algebras of finite global dimension, the related
Coxeter-polynomial formula in terms of boundary winding numbers is
given in \cite[Proposition~3.2]{ChangElaginSchroll}, following
\cite[Theorem~6.17]{JimenezGonzalezMroz}.

It remains to remove the sign \(\varepsilon\) in
\eqref{eq:reduced-serre-versus-rotation}. All eigenvalue multiplicities
in this proof are algebraic multiplicities.
For \(R_*^{-1}\), the multiplicities of the eigenvalues
\(1\) and \(-1\) are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sign-multiplicities}
 p_+=2g+2b-2,
 \qquad
 p_- =\#\{i:2\mid m_i\}.
\end{equation}
Multiplying the operator by \(-1\) interchanges these multiplicities.
If \(g\geq1\), then \(p_+\geq2b>p_-\). If \(g=0\) and \(b\geq3\),
then \(p_+=2b-2>b\geq p_-\). Among \(\mathsf C\) and
\(-\mathsf C\), the condition that the multiplicity of \(1\) is
larger therefore selects \(R_*^{-1}\) uniquely in these cases.

The remaining low-complexity possibilities are read from the same linear
data.  Once \(b=1\) is known, a disc is characterized by
\[
 \max\{\operatorname{mult}_{\mathsf C}(1),
        \operatorname{mult}_{\mathsf C}(-1)\}\leq1.
\]
Indeed, a positive-genus surface with one boundary component has
\(p_+=2g\geq2\), whereas a disc has at most one of the eigenvalues
\(\pm1\) on reduced zero-homology.  For a disc the stop number is recovered
directly as
\(
 m_1=1+\operatorname{rk} K\). If \(b=2\), equality of the two multiplicities in
\eqref{eq:sign-multiplicities} can occur only for an annulus whose two
stop numbers are even.  In that case,
 $(t^{m_1}-1)(t^{m_2}-1)$ is invariant under \(t\mapsto-t\), so either sign gives the same
characteristic polynomial.  Thus, outside the already reconstructed disc,
the conjugacy class of \(\mathsf C\) determines the polynomial on the right of
\eqref{eq:rotation-polynomial}.

For \(d\geq2\), let \(e_d\) be the multiplicity of the cyclotomic
polynomial \(\Phi_d(t)\) in \eqref{eq:rotation-polynomial}, and set
\[
 a_n=\#\{i:m_i=n\}.
\]
Since \(t^n-1=\prod_{d\mid n}\Phi_d(t)\), we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cyclotomic-counts}
 e_d=\sum_{d\mid n}a_n
 \qquad\text{ for }d\geq2.
\end{equation}
Let \(D=\max\{d\geq2:e_d\neq0\}\), with \(D=1\) if the set is empty.
Descending from \(D\) to \(2\), equation
\eqref{eq:cyclotomic-counts} recovers
\begin{equation*}
 a_n=e_n-\sum_{\substack{k\geq2\\kn\leq D}}a_{kn}
 \qquad\text{ for }n\geq2,
\end{equation*}
and then
\[
 a_1=b-\sum_{n=2}^D a_n.
\]
Hence the unordered stop distribution \(\{m_1,\ldots,m_b\}\) is determined.
In particular, the total number of stops
\(|M|=\sum_n n a_n\) is determined.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:rotation-polynomial-insufficient}
The nilpotent information is essential. Put one stop on each
boundary component of \(\Sigma_{1,2}\) and of \(\Sigma_{0,3}\).
For both surfaces, relative homology has rank four and the
boundary-rotation polynomial is \((t-1)^4\). However,
the ranks of \(R_*-I\) are one and two, respectively.
Thus the polynomial alone does not determine the number of
boundary components, whereas the rank used in
Lemma~\ref{lem:serre-recovers-boundary-number} distinguishes
these examples.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Let \(K=K(\mathbb S)\) and
\(\mathsf C=(\mathsf S[-1])_*\). Lemmas~\ref{lem:serre-recovers-boundary-number} and
\ref{lem:serre-recovers-stops} then recover \(b\), the unordered stop
distribution, and hence \(|M|\), from the rational conjugacy class of
\(\mathsf C\). The ranks in \eqref{eq:relative-homology-sequence} give
\begin{equation*}
 \operatorname{rk} K=2g+b+|M|-2,
 \qquad
 g=\frac{\operatorname{rk} K-b-|M|+2}{2}.
\end{equation*}
We have reconstructed the genus, the number of boundary components, and
the number of stops on every boundary component, up to permutation, which yields an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\((S,M)\simeq(S',M')\).  Every step used only the rational conjugacy class
of the Serre action, so an intertwining \(\mathbb Q\)-linear isomorphism gives the same
data on the two sides.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:general-boundary-reconstruction}
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) be a compact connected graded surface
of genus \(g\geq2\), with non-empty boundary and at least one stop on
each boundary component. Write \(b=|\pi_0(\partial S)|\), and suppose
that \(\mathcal W(\mathbb S)\) is smooth and proper. Let \(t\) be the number of
boundary components with odd winding. Then
\(\mathfrak R(\mathbb S)\) determines \((S,\eta)\) and \(|M|\). On
isomorphism classes of graded surfaces with stops subject to these
hypotheses, its fibre satisfies
\[
 \#\mathfrak R^{-1}([\mathfrak R(\mathbb S)])\leq
 \begin{cases}
 4,&b=4,\ t=4,\\
 2,&b=2,\ \text{or }(b=3,\ t=2),\\
 1,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
In particular, \(\mathfrak R\) determines the complete graded surface
with stops when \(b=1\) or \(b\geq5\).
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:represented-boundary-data}
Let \(g\geq2\) and \(b\geq2\). The represented invariant determines a
normalized action \(R:C\to\operatorname{Aut}(K)\), the absolute rational homology
space and its saturated integral lattice
\[
 A_{\mathbb Q}=K_{\mathbb Q}^{C}=H_1(S;L),
 \qquad
 A_{\mathbb Z}=K\cap A_{\mathbb Q}=H_1(S;L_{\mathbb Z}),
\]
and the unordered collection
\[
 \{(m_i,\nu_i):1\leq i\leq b\},
 \qquad m_i=|M\cap B_i|.
\]
If \(L\) is non-trivial, it also determines the rational endpoint
blocks, up to permutation, and the boundary-pointwise stabilizer
\[
 F=\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S,\partial S)}(\eta).
\]
For a specified primitive boundary-rotation direction \(r_i\), its
normalized action \(R_i\) satisfies
\[
 m_i=\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb Q}(R_i-I).
\]
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
By Theorem~\ref{thm:derived-reconstruction}, the represented invariant determines whether \(L\)
is trivial, and by Corollary~\ref{cor:geometric-reduction}, it determines
\(C\). By Lemma~\ref{lem:geometrization-charge-action}, its lifts act through
twisted relative homology and differ only by an overall sign. 

Suppose first that \(L\)
is trivial. Then the ordinary action of \(x\in C\) satisfies
\[
 \operatorname{tr}R(x)=2g+b-2+
 \#\operatorname{Fix}(x|_{M^\circ})>0.
\]
Positive trace therefore selects the ordinary action, and the selected
actions are multiplicative.

In this case, let \(x\in K_{\mathbb Q}\) be fixed by every
boundary rotation. On each boundary component, the endpoint
coefficients of \(x\) are constant. Invariance under the full twist
\(T_i\), together with \eqref{eq:boundary-twist-relative-homology},
gives \(\lambda_i(x)c_i=0\). Since \(b\geq2\), every boundary
class \(c_i\) is non-zero, so \(\lambda_i(x)=0\). Over
\(\mathbb Q\), the constant endpoint coefficients must therefore
all vanish. The relative homology sequence now gives
\(K_{\mathbb Q}^{C}=H_1(S;\mathbb Q)\). Conversely, boundary
rotations act trivially on absolute homology. The same exact sequence
over \(\mathbb Z\) shows that \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\) is saturated in \(K\).

We next construct the normalized action when \(L\) is non-trivial.
Let \(\widetilde C\) be the
full inverse image of \(C\) under \(D(\mathbb S)\to H_\eta\).
Its charge image is abelian: the collar-normalized rotations commute,
and all other lifts differ in their charge action only by an overall
sign. In the complex semisimplification of this representation of
\(\widetilde C\), let \(\chi_0\) denote the character of the action over
the core. Its multiplicity is \(\dim A_{\mathbb Q}+p\), where \(p\) is the number
of positive boundaries. Every other joint character occurs once, and its
ratio with \(\chi_0\) is supported on a single boundary coordinate. Thus
\(\chi_0\) is the unique joint character of multiplicity greater than
one. It takes values in \(\{1,-1\}\), is trivial on the geometrisation
kernel, and sends the shift to \(-1\).

For \(c\in C\), choose a lift
\(\widetilde c\in\widetilde C\) and set
\[
 R(c)=\chi_0(\widetilde c)^{-1}
       \varrho_{\mathbb S}(\widetilde c).
\]
This is independent of the lift and defines an intrinsic integral action
of \(C\) which is the identity on absolute homology. These assertions
follow from the signed-cycle calculation in
Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-twisted-collar} and the normal forms in
Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-homology-normal-forms}. The fixed space of the normalized action is \(A_{\mathbb Q}\). Indeed, a
negative endpoint block has no fixed vector. On a positive block, a
fixed endpoint vector has constant coefficients, and the full twist
forces its total to vanish. The exact sequence in
Lemma~\ref{lem:geometrization-charge-action} then identifies the
integral intersection with \(A_{\mathbb Z}\) and proves saturation.

We next record the endpoint data. For
\(x=\sum_i x_ir_i\in C_{\mathbb Q}\), choose \(d>0\) such that
\(dx_i\in m_i\mathbb Z\) on positive boundaries and
\(dx_i\in2m_i\mathbb Z\) on negative boundaries. For a positive boundary
\(B_i\), choose a local generator and put \(c_i=[B_i]_L\). Let
\(\lambda_i\) be the total endpoint coefficient on \(B_i\), and let
\(\varepsilon_\partial\in\{1,-1\}\) be the common collar sign. Then
\[
 \mathcal L(x)=d^{-1}\bigl(R(dx)-I\bigr)
 =\varepsilon_\partial
   \sum_{\nu_i=1}\frac{x_i}{m_i}c_i\otimes\lambda_i.
\]
The formula is independent of the sufficiently divisible choice of
\(d\) and defines a \(\mathbb Q\)-linear map on \(C_{\mathbb Q}\).
The positive boundary classes \(c_i\) are independent by
Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-homology-normal-forms}. The endpoint totals
\(\lambda_i\) are independent because \(H_0(S;L)=0\), so the endpoint
map onto \(H_0(M^\circ;L)\) is surjective. Factoring through their coordinate
spaces therefore gives
\[
 \operatorname{rk}\mathcal L\!\left(\sum_i x_i r_i\right)
 =\#\{i:\nu_i=1,\ x_i\neq0\}.
\]
Thus \(C_-:=\ker\mathcal L\) is the rational span of the negative
axes, and \(C\cap C_-\) is their primitive integral span. The
non-zero rank-one locus of \(\mathcal L\) on
\(C_{\mathbb Q}/C_-\) is precisely the union of the punctured
positive quotient axes.

Let \(\mathcal S\) be the set of non-trivial joint characters of \(R\).
On a positive block with \(m_i>1\), they form the non-trivial part of
a cyclic group \(X_i\) of order \(m_i\). On a negative block, they are the odd powers
of a character generating a group \(X_i\) of order \(2m_i\). The groups
\(X_i\) are exactly the maximal members of
\[
 \{\langle\chi\rangle:\chi\in\mathcal S\}.
\]
Indeed, a generator of each \(X_i\) occurs in \(\mathcal S\), every
occurring character is supported on a single boundary coordinate, and
the groups attached to different coordinates have trivial intersection.
Such a group comes from a negative boundary exactly when its restriction
to \(C\cap C_-\) is non-trivial. Its order therefore recovers \(2m_i\)
in the negative case and \(m_i\) in the positive case. The number of
boundaries supplies the positive one-point blocks which do not occur in
\(\mathcal S\). This recovers the pairs \((m_i,\nu_i)\). When \(L\) is
trivial, the same maximal-character argument recovers all \(m_i>1\),
and \(b\) supplies the remaining entries equal to one.

For non-trivial \(L\), put \(E=K_{\mathbb Q}/A_{\mathbb Q}\).
The action on \(E\) has finite image and is semisimple over
\(\mathbb Q\). A negative endpoint block is the sum of the character
