spaces belonging to the odd powers in \(X_i\). This sum is
Galois-stable and hence is defined over \(\mathbb Q\).

Each positive quotient axis gives a rank-one component of
\(\mathcal L\), whose kernel determines the corresponding endpoint
total \(\lambda_i\) up to a non-zero scalar. The map
\[
 E^C\longrightarrow\mathbb Q^p,
 \qquad v\longmapsto(\lambda_i(v))_{\nu_i=1},
\]
is an isomorphism: the fixed endpoint vectors have constant
coefficients on each positive boundary, and their totals may be
prescribed independently. The constant line associated with the
\(i\)-th positive quotient axis is therefore cut out by
\(\lambda_j=0\) for all other positive axes.

Each positive character group \(X_i\) is trivial on \(C\cap C_-\)
and hence descends to \(C/(C\cap C_-)\). Its restriction is
non-trivial on the primitive integral lattice of exactly one positive
quotient axis. This attaches its non-trivial character spaces to the
constant line associated with that axis. A positive axis on which no
such character occurs gives a one-point block. Thus every endpoint
block is recovered as a rational subspace, up to permutation.

Finally, the subgroup preserving every boundary component is
the centralizer of \(C\) in \(H_\eta\). An element of this subgroup lies
in \(F\) exactly when its projective action on every endpoint block is
trivial. Indeed, on the endpoint block at \(B_i\), the quotient action
is a scalar times a cyclic or signed cyclic permutation of the stops.
It is scalar precisely when the rotation exponent is divisible by
\(m_i\). This is the condition of fixing every stop, equivalently of
admitting a boundary-pointwise representative. It characterizes
\(F\) intrinsically, including the case \(m_i=1\). The last formula in
the statement is the rank calculation in
Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-twisted-collar}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
The last formula applies to a specified primitive rotation. It does not
identify the primitive axes from the rotation representation alone. On
a vector-framed surface with two boundary components and one stop on
each, the two twists act as \(I+U\), where \(U\neq0\) and \(U^2=0\).
Thus
\[
 (x,y)\longmapsto I+(x+y)U
\]
is preserved by changes of basis which do not permute the two axes.
The action of the line-field stabilizer and the integral charge
lattice impose further restrictions on this ambiguity. These are
precisely the restrictions used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:general-boundary-reconstruction}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:geometric-boundary-matching}
Assume that \(b\geq2\), and let \(\mathbb S'=(S',M',\eta')\) satisfy
the same hypotheses as \(\mathbb S\). Let
\((\Phi,\Theta):\mathfrak R(\mathbb S)\to\mathfrak R(\mathbb S')\) be
an isomorphism, and let \(f\) and \(\phi\) be supplied by
Corollary~\ref{cor:geometric-reduction}. After
choosing an integral identification of the orientation local systems,
\[
 \Theta|_{H_1(S;L_{\mathbb Z})}=\varepsilon f_*,
 \qquad \varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}.
\]
If \(L\) is trivial and \(b\geq3\), then \(f\) matches every boundary
component with a boundary having the same number of stops. If \(L\) is
non-trivial, the same conclusion holds for every positive boundary.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
The scalar assertion follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:absolute-intertwiner-scalar}.
Suppose first that \(L\) is trivial. For a sufficiently divisible
positive integer \(d\), the normalized action of \(dx\), with
\(x\in C_{\mathbb Q}\), defines
\[
 \mathcal L(x)=d^{-1}(R(dx)-I).
\]
For a common collar sign
\(\varepsilon_\partial\in\{1,-1\}\), the collar formula gives
\[
 \mathcal L\!\left(\sum_i x_ir_i\right)
 =\varepsilon_\partial
  \sum_i\frac{x_i}{m_i}c_i\otimes\lambda_i,
\]
where \(c_i=[B_i]\) and \(\lambda_i\) is the endpoint total on
\(B_i\). The only relations in these two families are
\(\sum_i c_i=0\) and \(\sum_i\lambda_i=0\). If \(b\geq3\), these
relations show that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary-axis-from-absolute-class}
 \{x\in C_{\mathbb Q}:\operatorname{im}\mathcal L(x)
       \subseteq\mathbb Qc_i\}=\mathbb Qr_i.
\end{equation}
To verify this identity, write
\(\mathcal L(x)=\sum_j a_jc_j\otimes\lambda_j\) and reduce modulo
\(\mathbb Qc_i\). The functionals \(\lambda_j\) with \(j\neq i\)
are independent coordinates, since the only relation among all
endpoint totals is their sum. Each \(c_j\), for \(j\neq i\),
remains non-zero modulo \(\mathbb Qc_i\) when \(b\geq3\). Thus
\(a_j=0\) for every \(j\neq i\) and the converse is immediate from the
collar formula.

The absolute intertwiner carries \(\mathbb Qc_i\) to
\(\mathbb Qf_*c_i\). Equation~\eqref{eq:boundary-axis-from-absolute-class}
and equivariance of \(\mathcal L\) therefore show that \(\phi\) carries
\(\mathbb Qr_i\) to the rotation axis at \(f(B_i)\).
Proposition~\ref{prop:represented-boundary-data} then recovers \(m_i\)
from the primitive integral generator.

Now suppose that \(L\) is non-trivial. For each positive boundary, put
\(c_i=[B_i]_L\), let \(\lambda_i\) be its endpoint-total covector, and
choose the common collar sign
\(\varepsilon_\partial\in\{1,-1\}\). The same logarithm is
\[
 \mathcal L\!\left(\sum_i x_ir_i\right)
 =\varepsilon_\partial
  \sum_{\nu_i=1}\frac{x_i}{m_i}c_i\otimes\lambda_i.
\]
The positive boundary classes and the endpoint totals are independent.
Put
\[
 C_-=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}\{r_i:\nu_i=-1\}.
\]
It follows that the kernel is \(C_-\) and
that the non-zero rank-one locus in \(C_{\mathbb Q}/C_-\) is the union of
the punctured positive axes. The scalar assertion matches each positive boundary
line with its geometric counterpart. Equivariance of \(\mathcal L\)
therefore matches the corresponding positive axis. The cyclic
characters in Proposition~\ref{prop:represented-boundary-data} recover
its stop number, including the case of one stop.

Once the relevant stop numbers have been matched, an orientation
correction from Lemma~\ref{lem:orientation-correction} may be applied
before the final boundary-collar isotopies. It preserves every boundary
component and the line-field homotopy class.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
The shift-pointed part of \(\mathfrak R\) and
Theorem~\ref{thm:derived-reconstruction} recover \((S,\eta)\), as well
as \(g\) and \(b\). The rank formula gives
\[
 |M|=\operatorname{rk} K(\mathbb S)-2g-b+2.
\]
For \(b=1\), this is the only stop number. For \(b=2\),
Proposition~\ref{prop:represented-boundary-data} recovers the unordered
pair of stop numbers, leaving at most two assignments.

Suppose \(b\geq3\). If \(L\) is trivial,
Proposition~\ref{prop:geometric-boundary-matching} matches every
boundary. If \(L\) is non-trivial, the same proposition matches all
positive boundaries. When \(t=0\), this completes the reconstruction.
When \(t=2\) and \(b\geq4\), there are at least two positive
boundaries, and Lemma~\ref{lem:integral-negative-boundary-matching} \textup{(ii)}
excludes the exchange of the two negative blocks. For \(b=3\), only
that exchange remains, giving at most two assignments.

If \(t\geq4\), the mod-two pair labels in
Lemma~\ref{lem:integral-negative-boundary-matching} \textup{(i)} fix every negative
block unless \(b=t=4\). In the latter case the remaining permutations
belong to the Klein four group and give at most four assignments.
Repeated stop counts and graded automorphisms can only reduce these
bounds. Whenever all counts match, an orientation correction followed
by collar isotopies identifies the graded surfaces with stops.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
\label{ex:two-boundary-represented-failure}
For every \(g\geq2\), let \(S=\Sigma_{g,2}\), and put \(m=2g+1\) and
\(q=4g+1\). Consider a line field \(\eta\) on \(S\) with boundary
windings \((1,-(4g+1))\). Such a line field can be constructed by
projectivizing a vector framing with boundary windings \((0,-2g)\) and
then adding an integral cohomology class with boundary evaluations
\((1,-1)\) and zero handle evaluations. Since the two boundary windings
are distinct, every element of the line-field stabilizer \(H_\eta\)
preserves each boundary component. Hence \(H_\eta=H_\eta^0\).

We now consider the two assignments of stop counts to the ordered boundary
components \((B_1,B_2)\): $(1,m)$ and $(m,1)$. The two resulting graded surfaces with stops have isomorphic represented
invariants, but their partially wrapped Fukaya categories are not
equivalent.
Let \(F\) be the boundary-pointwise stabilizer. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:relative-displacement}, relative displacement
gives a character
\[
 \beta:F\longrightarrow\mathbb Z,
 \qquad \beta(T_1)=1,\quad\beta(T_2)=q.
\]
The central element \(k=T_1^{-q}T_2\) lies in \(\ker\beta\), and
\(\alpha(y)=yk^{\beta(y)}\) is an automorphism of \(F\). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:stopped-stabilizer-root-presentation}, the stopped
stabilizers are obtained by adjoining \(\rho_2^m=T_2\) and
\((\rho'_1)^m=T_1\). The formula
\[
 \phi|_F=\alpha,
 \qquad
 \phi(\rho_2)=(\rho'_1)^{-q^2}T_2^2
\]
extends to an isomorphism and admits an integral intertwiner of the
charge representations by
Lemma~\ref{lem:exceptional-representation-calculations} \textup{(i)}. Hence the
two invariants \(\mathfrak R\) are isomorphic.

On the other hand, the boundary windings are distinct, so no graded
diffeomorphism can exchange the two boundary components.
Theorem~\ref{thm:complete-surface-invariant} therefore implies that the
two partially wrapped Fukaya categories are not equivalent. Their common
Grothendieck rank is \(4g+2\).
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- example-5: body_md -->
\label{ex:three-boundary-represented-failure}
Let \(S=\Sigma_{g,3}\), where \(g\geq2\), and let \(m>1\). There is a
line field \(\eta\) on \(S\) with boundary windings
\(
 (1,-1,-(4g+2))
\)
for which the ordered stop counts \((1,m,1)\) and \((m,1,1)\)
on \((B_1,B_2,B_3)\) give graded surfaces with stops having isomorphic
represented invariants and non-equivalent categories.

Begin with a vector-framed line field of boundary windings
\((0,0,-(4g+2))\), and add a class with boundary evaluations
\((1,-1,0)\) and zero handle evaluations. The three resulting boundary
windings are distinct, so \(H_\eta=H_\eta^0\). Let \(F\) be the
boundary-pointwise stabilizer, and set
\[
 L_\partial=
 \ker\bigl(H^1(S,\partial S;\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\bigr).
\]
Let \(\beta_\eta:F\to L_\partial\) be the relative-displacement map of
Lemma~\ref{lem:relative-displacement}, and let \(e_1-e_2\) denote the
coordinate-difference homomorphism on \(L_\partial\). With the common
sign \(\varepsilon_0\) from that lemma, put
\[
 h=\varepsilon_0(e_1-e_2)\circ\beta_\eta:F\longrightarrow\mathbb Z.
\]
Then
\[
 h(T_1)=h(T_2)=1,
 \qquad h(T_3)=0.
\]
Set \(k=T_1^{-1}T_2\) and \(q=2m-1\). The element \(k\) is central and
lies in \(\ker h\). The map \(\alpha(y)=yk^{qh(y)}\) is an automorphism
of \(F\). The additional
roots are \(\rho_2^m=T_2\) and \((\rho'_1)^m=T_1\), and
\[
 \phi|_F=\alpha,
 \qquad
 \phi(\rho_2)=(\rho'_1)^{-q}T_2^2.
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:exceptional-representation-calculations} \textup{(ii)} gives the
integral intertwiner and hence the isomorphism of \(\mathfrak R\).
The distinct windings exclude a graded boundary exchange, so the
categories are not equivalent by
Theorem~\ref{thm:complete-surface-invariant}. Their Grothendieck rank
is \(2g+m+3\). In particular, \(g=3\) and \(m=2\) give rank \(11\).
<!-- end example-5 -->
<!-- example-6: body_md -->
\label{ex:four-boundary-represented-failure}
Let \(S=\Sigma_{g,4}\), where \(g\geq2\), and choose \(m,n>1\) with
\(m+n=2g+2\). There is a line field \(\eta\) on \(S\) with boundary windings
\[
 (1,-(2m-1),-1,-(2n+1))
\]
for which \((1,m,1,n)\) and \((m,1,n,1)\) have isomorphic represented
invariants but non-equivalent categories.

The four winding numbers are pairwise distinct. Indeed, the only
possible remaining equality would force \(m=n+1\), making \(m+n\)
odd and contradicting \(m+n=2g+2\). Hence \(H_\eta=H_\eta^0\).

Start with a line field induced by a vector framing and having boundary
winding numbers \((0,-2m,0,-2n)\). Add a class with boundary evaluations
\((1,1,-1,-1)\) and zero handle evaluations. Put \(q_m=2m-1\) and
\(q_n=2n+1\). For the boundary-pointwise stabilizer \(F\),
represent the relative displacement of \(y\in F\) by
\[
 (e_1(y),e_2(y),e_3(y),e_4(y))
 \in\mathbb Z^4/\mathbb Z(1,1,1,1).
\]
Let \(\varepsilon_0\) be the common sign in
Lemma~\ref{lem:relative-displacement}, and set
\[
 h_{12}=\varepsilon_0(e_1-e_2),
 \qquad h_{34}=\varepsilon_0(e_3-e_4).
\]
These characters have values
\[
 \begin{array}{c|rrrr}
  &T_1&T_2&T_3&T_4\\\hline
  h_{12}&1&q_m&0&0\\
  h_{34}&0&0&-1&q_n.
 \end{array}
\]
Set
\[
 k_{12}=T_1^{-q_m}T_2,
 \qquad k_{34}=T_3^{-q_n}T_4^{-1},
\]
and
\[
 \alpha(y)=y k_{12}^{h_{12}(y)}k_{34}^{h_{34}(y)}.
\]
The elements \(k_{12}\) and \(k_{34}\) are central, and both characters
vanish on them. Hence \(\alpha\) is an automorphism of \(F\). Let
\(\rho_2,\rho_4\) be the source rotations and
\(\rho'_1,\rho'_3\) the target rotations. Their root relations are
\[
 \rho_2^m=T_2,\qquad \rho_4^n=T_4,
 \qquad (\rho'_1)^m=T_1,\qquad (\rho'_3)^n=T_3.
\]
The stopped stabilizers are related by
\[
 \phi|_F=\alpha,
 \qquad
 \phi(\rho_2)=(\rho'_1)^{-q_m^2}T_2^2,
 \qquad
 \phi(\rho_4)=(\rho'_3)^{-q_n^2}T_4^{-2}.
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:exceptional-representation-calculations} \textup{(iii)} proves that
this isomorphism admits an integral charge intertwiner. Thus the two
represented invariants agree. The distinct winding numbers prevent a
graded boundary exchange. The categories are therefore not equivalent
by Theorem~\ref{thm:complete-surface-invariant} and their common
Grothendieck rank is \(4g+6\).
<!-- end example-6 -->
<!-- example-7: body_md -->
\label{ex:represented-low-genus-failure}
The genus hypothesis in
Theorem~\ref{thm:general-boundary-reconstruction} cannot be removed.
For genus zero, use the pair of pants in
Example~\ref{ex:low-genus-failure}. Its two line fields have even
boundary winding numbers and hence are induced by vector fields.
Normalize graded lifts at the same stop in both models. In the
trivializations supplied by these vector fields, the induced
local-system isomorphism has sign \(+1\) at that stop and therefore
everywhere. Thus corresponding boundary twists act identically on
\(H_1(\Sigma_{0,3},M^\circ;\mathbb Z)\). In the common presentation
\eqref{eq:genus-zero-common-group}, take the identity on the
local-system torus, the boundary-twist lattice, and the shift factor.
The torus acts trivially on the charge lattice and the shift acts by
\(-I\). This group identification, together with the identity on
ordinary relative homology, intertwines the full integral
representations.

For genus one, use the notation of the same example and compare
\(\eta_0+2\alpha\) with \(\eta_0+4\alpha\). Both line fields are induced
by vector fields, and their stabilizer is the same group \(H_\alpha\).
Normalize the graded lifts at the common unique stop. In the
trivializations supplied by the vector fields, their induced maps on
the orientation local systems have sign \(+1\) at this stop and
therefore everywhere. Thus the normalized lifts in both models act
on ordinary relative homology by the same underlying diffeomorphisms.
The shift acts by \(-I\) and the local-system torus acts trivially.
\enlargethispage*{2\baselineskip}
The common group identification consequently intertwines the two
integral representations. Thus their invariants \(\mathfrak R\) are
isomorphic. Nevertheless,
\[
 \widetilde{\mathcal A}(\eta_0+2\alpha)=2,
 \qquad
 \widetilde{\mathcal A}(\eta_0+4\alpha)=4.
\]
The line-field classification distinguishes both pairs of graded surfaces.
<!-- end example-7 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:relative-displacement}
The map \(\beta_\eta\) is a homomorphism
\[
 \beta_\eta:\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S,\partial S)}(\eta)\longrightarrow L_\partial
 \simeq\mathbb Z^b/\mathbb Z(1,\ldots,1).
\]
There is a sign \(\varepsilon_0\in\{1,-1\}\), independent of \(i\), such
that every vector framing \(\xi\), viewed relative to its boundary
restriction, satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:relative-boundary-twist-framing}
 [T_i^*\xi]-[\xi]
 =\varepsilon_0\omega_\xi(B_i)u_i.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\[
 \beta_\eta(T_i)=\varepsilon_0\omega_\eta(B_i)u_i.
\]
If \(\beta_\eta(y)\) is represented by \((d_1,\ldots,d_b)\), then
\[
 \delta_i(y)\delta_j(y)=(-1)^{d_i-d_j}.
\]
Thus the \(B_i\)- and \(B_j\)-normalized charge actions differ by
\((-1)^{d_i-d_j}\).
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Relative homotopy classes of framings with prescribed boundary value
form a torsor over \(H^1(S,\partial S;\mathbb Z)\). Since a boundary twist is
isotopic to the identity after the boundary condition is forgotten,
the difference \([T_i^*\xi]-[\xi]\) lies in \(L_\partial\).

Choose basepoints on the boundary and transverse arcs from \(B_1\)
to the other boundary components, with fixed tangent directions at
their endpoints. Together with an absolute homology basis, these arcs
detect a relative framing \cite[Lemma~2.2]{CalderonSalter}. Changing
the endpoint conventions adds constants that cancel in the difference
before and after a boundary twist. The twist-linearity formula
\cite[Lemma~2.4]{CalderonSalter} gives
\[
 \omega_\xi(T_i(a))-\omega_\xi(a)
 =\langle a,B_i\rangle\omega_\xi(B_i)
\]
for every such oriented arc \(a\). The functional
\(a\mapsto\langle a,B_i\rangle\) is, up to the orientation convention
common to all boundary components, the evaluation of \(u_i\). This proves
\eqref{eq:relative-boundary-twist-framing}.

Relative homotopy classes of line fields with prescribed boundary
value form the analogous torsor. Since an element of \(\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S,\partial S)}(\eta)\) preserves
the absolute homotopy class of \(\eta\), its relative displacement lies
in \(L_\partial\). The cohomology sequence of the pair identifies this
group with \(\mathbb Z^b/\mathbb Z(1,\ldots,1)\). The boundary-pointwise mapping class
group acts trivially on this quotient, so the displacement crossed
homomorphism is a homomorphism. The preceding arc calculation applies
to tangent lines and proves the stated twist formula.

At a fixed point of \(B_i\), an absolute homotopy from \(y^*\eta\) to
\(\eta\) is a loop in the projective tangent line. Differences of its
degrees on two boundary components represent the coordinate differences
of \(\beta_\eta(y)\). A loop of degree one in
\(\mathbb P^1(\mathbb R)\) reverses its oriented-line lift. This proves
the sign formula and the final assertion.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:geometrization-charge-action}
The geometrisation kernel \(N(\mathbb S)\) acts trivially on
\(K(\mathbb S)\). Under the identification of
Theorem~\ref{thm:charge-lattice}, a graded mapping class acts through
the induced map on twisted relative homology. Changing its graded lift
by \([n]\) multiplies this action by \((-1)^n\).

There is also a natural exact sequence
\[
 0\longrightarrow H_1(S;\mathbb Z_\eta)
 \longrightarrow K(\mathbb S)
 \longrightarrow
 \ker\bigl(H_0(M^\circ;\mathbb Z_\eta)\longrightarrow
                 H_0(S;\mathbb Z_\eta)\bigr)
 \longrightarrow0.
\]
In particular, \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z_\eta)\) is saturated in
\(K(\mathbb S)\).
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Put \(N=N(\mathbb S)\) and \(K=K(\mathbb S)\). By
Theorem~\ref{thm:opper-geometrization}, we may write
\[
 N=U\rtimes T,\qquad
 U\simeq(\mathbf{k},+)^{\phi_A(1,1)},\qquad
 T=H^1(S;\mathbf{k}^\times).
\]
Both \(U\) and \(T\) are divisible abelian groups. This uses
characteristic zero for \(U\), and algebraic closedness together with
the freeness of \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\) for \(T\). A homomorphism from a
divisible group to a finite group \(G\) is trivial, since every image
element is a \(|G|\)-th power in \(G\). Fix a prime \(\ell\).
For every \(n\geq1\), the induced homomorphism
\[
 N\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(K/\ell^nK)
\]
has finite target. Its restrictions to \(U\) and \(T\) are therefore
trivial. These subgroups generate \(N\), so the entire homomorphism is
trivial. Thus, for \(z\in N\) and \(x\in K\),
\[
 (\varrho_{\mathbb S}(z)-I)x
 \in\bigcap_{n\geq1}\ell^nK=0,
\]
because \(K\) is free abelian of finite rank. This proves that \(N\)
acts trivially on \(K\).

The geometric section of the geometrisation homomorphism
\cite[Theorem~7.1]{Opper2025}, together with the naturality of
Theorem~\ref{thm:charge-lattice}, identifies the resulting quotient
action with the induced action on twisted relative homology. A
generator of the deck group represents the shift and acts by \(-I\)
on the Grothendieck group. Changing a graded lift by \([n]\) therefore
multiplies its action by \((-1)^n\).

The exact sequence in the statement is the homology sequence of the
pair \((S,M^\circ)\). Its quotient is a subgroup of the free group
\(H_0(M^\circ;\mathbb Z_\eta)\), and is therefore free. This also proves the
saturation assertion.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:no-normal-abelian-subgroup}
Let \(X\) be a closed oriented surface of genus at least two and let
\(Q\neq\varnothing\) be finite.  A finite-index subgroup of
\(\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)\) has no non-trivial normal abelian subgroup.  Moreover, the
centralizer in \(\operatorname{Mod}(X,Q)\) of a finite-index subgroup of
\(\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)\) is trivial.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Let \(\Gamma\leq\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)\) have finite index and let
\(A\triangleleft\Gamma\) be abelian. Choose a finite-index normal
subgroup \(\Gamma_0\triangleleft\Gamma\) contained in a torsion-free
pure finite-index subgroup of the mapping class group, and put
\(B=A\cap\Gamma_0\).

Suppose that \(B\neq1\). Its canonical reduction system is preserved
by \(\Gamma\), since \(B\) is normal in \(\Gamma\). This system
cannot be non-empty. Indeed, a power of a Dehn twist about a curve
intersecting it belongs to \(\Gamma\) and does not preserve it. A non-trivial pure
abelian subgroup with empty reduction system contains a pseudo-Anosov
element. In this case \(\Gamma\) preserves the pair of its invariant
measured foliations up to scale. The stabilizer of this pair is virtually
cyclic, which is impossible for a finite-index subgroup of
\(\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)\). See \cite{BirmanLubotzkyMcCarthy,FarbMargalit}.
Thus \(B=1\), and \(A\) is finite.

The kernel \(\Gamma_1\) of the conjugation action of \(\Gamma\) on \(A\)
has finite index in \(\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)\).  It contains a non-zero power of the
twist about every essential curve.  Every element of \(A\) centralizes
\(\Gamma_1\), and hence fixes every vertex of the curve complex.  The
action on that complex is faithful in the present punctured range
\cite{Ivanov1997,Luo}.  Thus \(A=1\), proving the first assertion.  The
same curve-complex argument applies directly to any element centralizing a
finite-index subgroup of \(\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)\), and proves the second assertion.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:psi-classes}
The classes \(\psi_1,\ldots,\psi_b\) are linearly independent. Moreover,
\[
 c_f^*\psi'_{\pi(i)}=\varepsilon\psi_i,
 \qquad \varepsilon\in\{1,-1\},
\]
where \(\varepsilon=1\) if \(f\) preserves orientation and
\(\varepsilon=-1\) otherwise.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Let \(\mathcal M_{g,b}\subset\overline{\mathcal M}_{g,b}\) be the moduli
stack of smooth genus-\(g\) curves with \(b\) ordered marked points and
its stable compactification. The degree-two localization sequence contains
the exact segment
\[
 \bigoplus_{\Delta\subset
  \overline{\mathcal M}_{g,b}\setminus\mathcal M_{g,b}}
 \mathbb Q[\Delta]
 \longrightarrow H^2(\overline{\mathcal M}_{g,b};\mathbb Q)
 \longrightarrow H^2(\mathcal M_{g,b};\mathbb Q).
\]
Thus the kernel of restriction from the compactification is spanned by
boundary-divisor classes.
Hence a relation among the \(\psi_i\) on \(\mathcal M_{g,b}\) would give
a relation among those classes and the boundary divisors on
\(\overline{\mathcal M}_{g,b}\).

For \(g>2\), \cite[Theorem~2.2]{ArbarelloCornalba} gives no such
relation, apart from the symmetry identifying two names for the same
boundary divisor. In genus two, the additional relation in
\cite[Theorem~2.2]{ArbarelloCornalba} has non-zero coefficient of the
first Mumford class. It therefore gives no non-zero
relation involving only the \(\psi_i\) and boundary-divisor classes.
This proves independence in every genus \(g\geq2\). Indeed, Teichm\"uller
space \(\mathcal T_{g,b}\) is contractible and
\(\mathcal M_{g,b}^{\mathrm{an}}=[\mathcal T_{g,b}/\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)]\).
Thus the analytic stack is a classifying orbifold for
\(\operatorname{PMod}(X,Q)\).

The final assertion follows from the naturality of the cotangent-line
classes. If \(L_i\) and \(L'_{\pi(i)}\) denote the corresponding complex
cotangent lines, then an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism identifies
\(f^*L'_{\pi(i)}\) with \(L_i\). An orientation-reversing diffeomorphism
identifies it with \(\overline{L_i}\). Since
\(c_1(\overline{L_i})=-c_1(L_i)\), all cotangent-line classes acquire the
same sign.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:separating-fixer}
Let \(S=\Sigma_{g,b}\) with \(g\geq2\), and let \(M\) meet every boundary
component. If \(\mathcal K_{\mathrm{sep}}\leq\operatorname{Mod}(S,M)\) is the subgroup fixing the
isotopy class of every separating essential curve, then the
boundary-rotation lattice \(A_M\) has finite index in
\(\mathcal K_{\mathrm{sep}}\).
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Use the capping map \(\kappa\) from
\eqref{eq:coloured-capping-map}.  Its kernel is \(A_M\), and
\(\kappa(\mathcal K_{\mathrm{sep}})\) fixes every vertex of the separating-curve
complex of the capped surface \((\widehat S,Q)\).  If \(g\geq3\), or if
\(g=2\) and \(b\geq2\), the natural extended mapping-class-group action
on this complex is faithful by \cite[Theorem~1.2]{Kida}. Thus the image is
trivial in these cases.

It remains to consider \((g,b)=(2,1)\). Choose a genus-one
separating curve \(c\) on the once-punctured genus-two
surface and a pseudo-Anosov mapping class \(f\). For
sufficiently large \(n\), the curves \(c\) and \(f^n(c)\)
fill the surface because their distance in the curve graph eventually
exceeds two. Both are genus-one separating curves.
By the Alexander method, the subgroup fixing the isotopy
class of every curve in this filling collection is finite (see \cite{FarbMargalit}).
Since
\(\kappa(\mathcal K_{\mathrm{sep}})\) fixes every separating curve, it is contained in
this finite stabilizer.  Hence
\(\mathcal K_{\mathrm{sep}}/A_M
\simeq\kappa(\mathcal K_{\mathrm{sep}})\) is finite in all cases.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:orientation-correction}
Let \(S\) be a compact connected oriented surface with non-empty boundary
and \(g(S)\geq2\). Every line field \(\eta\) on \(S\) is preserved up to homotopy
by an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism that preserves every boundary
component setwise.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
By the classification of compact surfaces, there is an
orientation-reversing diffeomorphism \(r:S\to S\) which preserves every
boundary component setwise.  Put
\(\theta=r^*\eta\).  The orientation of \(S\) orients the fibres of
\(\mathbb P(TS)\).  Since \(r\) reverses this fibre orientation, for every
oriented immersed closed curve \(c\) one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:orientation-reversal-winding}
 \omega_\theta(c)=-\omega_\eta(r(c)).
\end{equation}
If \(B\subset\partial S\) is given its boundary orientation, then
\(r(B)=-B\) as oriented curves.  Consequently
\[
 \omega_\theta(B)=\omega_\eta(B).
\]

We compare the remaining invariants in the classification of mapping-class
orbits of line fields.  Reduction of
\eqref{eq:orientation-reversal-winding} modulo \(2\) shows that the mod-two
winding class of \(\theta\) is obtained from that of \(\eta\) by the
isomorphism induced by \(r\).  It is therefore zero if and only if the
mod-two winding class of \(\eta\) is zero.  In the case in which the Arf
invariant is defined, let \(q_\eta\) and \(q_\theta\) be the associated
\(\mathbb Z/4\)-valued quadratic functions.  Formula~\eqref{eq:orientation-reversal-winding} gives
\[
 q_\theta(x)=-q_\eta(r_*x)
 \qquad \text{ for }x\in H_1(S;\mathbb Z/4).
\]
Both functions then take values in \(2\mathbb Z/4\), on which multiplication
by \(-1\) is the identity.  After division by \(2\), they descend to
quadratic forms on the homology of the capped surface.  The map \(r_*\)
preserves the mod-two intersection form, because the distinction between
symplectic and anti-symplectic disappears over \(\mathbb Z/2\).  The two
quadratic forms therefore have the same Arf invariant.

Thus \(\eta\) and \(\theta\) have the same boundary winding numbers, the same
mod-two invariant, and, when required, the same Arf invariant.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:line-field-classification}, whose mapping class group fixes
the boundary pointwise, there is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\(h:S\to S\), equal to the identity on \(\partial S\), such that
\(h^*\theta\simeq\eta\).  The composition
\(
 \rho=r\circ h
\)
is orientation reversing, preserves every boundary component setwise, and
satisfies
\[
 \rho^*\eta=h^*r^*\eta=h^*\theta\simeq\eta.
\]
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:twists-in-chillingworth-kernel}
Assume that \(g\geq3\), or that \(g=2\) and \(b\geq2\), and put
\(r=2g+b-3\). For \(1\neq x\in\mathcal C_0(S)\), the following are
equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item \(Z(C_{\mathcal C_0(S)}(x))\simeq\mathbb Z\), and
\(x\in A\leq\mathcal C_0(S)\) for some \(A\simeq\mathbb Z^r\).
\item \(x\) is a non-zero power of a Dehn twist about a separating curve.
\end{enumerate}
Hence every isomorphism \(\mathcal C_0(S)\to\mathcal C_0(S')\) in this
range preserves the cyclic axes \(\langle T_c\rangle\) of separating
twists.
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
For any other line field \(\theta\) on \(S\), write
\(\theta-\eta=\alpha\in H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\). If
\(x\in\mathcal I_0(S)\), then
\[
 x^*\theta-\theta=(x^*\eta-\eta)+(x^*\alpha-\alpha)
 =x^*\eta-\eta.
\]
This proves the independence of \(\eta\). Separating twists act
trivially on absolute homology and preserve every line field, so
\(\mathcal K(S)\subseteq\mathcal C_0(S)\). To prove the other
inclusion, cap one boundary component and denote the resulting surface
by \(R\), with capping homomorphism
\(q:\operatorname{PMod}(S)\to\operatorname{PMod}(R)\). By the Birman exact sequence,
\(\ker q\) is generated by point-pushing maps. Pushing the capped
point around a handle generator gives a bounding-pair map, while
pushing it around another boundary component gives a product of
separating twists. Thus \(\ker q\subseteq\mathcal I(S)\);
see \cite[Section~4.2]{FarbMargalit} and the conventions in
\cite[Section~2]{Kida}. The separating twists and bounding-pair
maps generating \(\mathcal I(R)\) admit lifts of the same type,
and capping sends every generator of \(\mathcal I(S)\) into
\(\mathcal I(R)\). Hence
\[
 q(\mathcal I(S))=\mathcal I(R),
 \qquad
 \mathcal I(S)=q^{-1}(\mathcal I(R)).
\]
Iterating and using the homological description of the Torelli group
of the closed surface shows that \(\mathcal I_0(S)\subseteq
\mathcal I(S)\). Consequently,
\[
 \mathcal K(S)\subseteq\mathcal C_0(S)\subseteq\mathcal I(S).
\]

By \cite[Proposition~6.3]{Kida}, the maximal rank of an abelian subgroup of both
\(\mathcal K(S)\) and \(\mathcal I(S)\) is \(r\).  The inclusions above therefore show that the
same is true for \(\mathcal C_0(S)\).

Suppose first that \(x=T_c^n\), where \(c\) is separating
and \(n\neq0\). Apply \cite[Lemma~6.5]{Kida} with
\(\Gamma=\mathcal K(S)\). It follows that
\(
 Z(C_{\mathcal K(S)}(x))\simeq\mathbb Z
\)
and that \(x\) lies in a free abelian subgroup of
\(\mathcal K(S)\) of rank \(r\). The latter subgroup is
also contained in \(\mathcal C_0(S)\).

The groups \(\mathcal K(S)\) and \(\mathcal C_0(S)\)
have the same maximal abelian rank \(r\), and every abelian
subgroup of \(\mathcal C_0(S)\) is finitely generated
\cite{BirmanLubotzkyMcCarthy}.
The rank-\(r\) subgroup just constructed contains \(x\), so the
remaining hypothesis of \cite[Lemma~6.8]{Kida} is also satisfied.
Applying that lemma to
\(\mathcal K(S)\hookrightarrow\mathcal C_0(S)\), we obtain
\[
 \operatorname{rk} Z(C_{\mathcal C_0(S)}(x))
 \leq \operatorname{rk} Z(C_{\mathcal K(S)}(x))=1.
\]
The left-hand centre contains the infinite-order element \(x\).
It is finitely generated by \cite{BirmanLubotzkyMcCarthy} and
is torsion free by \cite[Theorem~A.1]{Kida}. Hence it is isomorphic
to \(\mathbb Z\). This proves
\textup{(b)}\(\Rightarrow\)\textup{(a)}.

Conversely, assume \textup{(a)}.  Apply \cite[Lemma~6.8]{Kida} to the
inclusion
\(
 \mathcal C_0(S)\hookrightarrow\mathcal I(S)\). The domain and codomain have the same finite maximal abelian rank \(r\),
and abelian subgroups of the codomain are finitely generated
\cite{BirmanLubotzkyMcCarthy}.
Condition~\textup{(a)} supplies a rank-\(r\) free abelian subgroup
of the domain containing \(x\), as required by the lemma. It gives
\[
 \operatorname{rk} Z(C_{\mathcal I(S)}(x))
 \leq \operatorname{rk} Z(C_{\mathcal C_0(S)}(x))=1.
\]
The left-hand centre contains \(x\), and \(\mathcal I(S)\) is torsion free
by \cite[Theorem~A.1]{Kida}.
Thus it is infinite cyclic.  The rank-\(r\) subgroup of
\(\mathcal C_0(S)\) containing \(x\) is also a rank-\(r\) subgroup of
\(\mathcal I(S)\). \cite[Lemma~6.6]{Kida} now implies that \(x\) is a non-zero power either
of a separating twist or of a bounding-pair twist.

The second possibility is excluded by the two defining conditions of
\(\mathcal C_0(S)\).  Suppose that
\(x=(T_aT_b^{-1})^n\in\mathcal C_0(S)\), where \(n\neq0\).  The homological
action of a bounding-pair map is integral and unipotent.  If its non-zero
power acts trivially, then the map itself acts trivially.  Thus
\(T_aT_b^{-1}\in\mathcal I_0(S)\), and \(a,b\) may be oriented so that
\([a]=[b]\).  The relation obtained
from a component of \(S\setminus(a\cup b)\) then shows that one of the two
components contains no component of \(\partial S\).  Indeed, among the
boundary classes, the only relation is the sum of all of them.  Choose that
component and denote its genus by \(h\).  Since \(a\) and \(b\) are not
isotopic, \(h\geq1\).  Formulae
\eqref{eq:twist-line-field} and
\eqref{eq:bounding-pair-winding-difference} give
\[
 \bigl((T_aT_b^{-1})^n\bigr)^*\eta-\eta
 =n\bigl(\omega_\eta(a)-\omega_\eta(b)\bigr)a^\vee
 =-4hn\,a^\vee\neq0
\]
for \(n\neq0\).  Hence no non-zero power of a bounding-pair twist belongs
to \(\mathcal C_0(S)\), and \textup{(a)} implies \textup{(b)}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:S21-two-torsion}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:S21-image-orders}
 |R_\eta|=
 \begin{cases}
 48,&\sigma(\eta)=1,\\
 72,&\sigma(\eta)=0\text{ and }\operatorname{Arf}(\eta)=0,\\
 120,&\sigma(\eta)=0\text{ and }\operatorname{Arf}(\eta)=1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Consequently, \(|R_\eta|\) determines the mapping-class orbit of \(\eta\).
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
The Poincar\'e--Hopf formula gives \(\omega_\eta(\partial S)=-6\). The mod-two
intersection pairing identifies \(H_1(S;\mathbb F_2)\) equivariantly
with its dual. We may therefore compute the representation on
\(V=H_1(S;\mathbb F_2)\), whose intersection form is symplectic. Moreover,
\[
 |\operatorname{Sp}(V)|
 =|\operatorname{Sp}_4(\mathbb F_2)|=720.
\]
If \(\sigma(\eta)=1\), reduction of the winding function gives a non-zero
linear form on \(V\).  There are fifteen such forms, and
\(\operatorname{Sp}_4(\mathbb F_2)\) acts transitively on them.  The
stabilizer therefore has order \(720/15=48\).

If \(\sigma(\eta)=0\), the \(\mathbb Z/4\)-valued quadratic function of
the line field is even and, after division by two, gives a quadratic form
on \(V\).  There are ten even and six odd quadratic forms on a symplectic
four-space.  Their stabilizers therefore have orders \(720/10=72\) and
\(720/6=120\), respectively.

We must still prove that \(R_\eta\) is the full stabilizer of the indicated
datum.  The homological representation
\(\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{2,1})\to\operatorname{Sp}_4(\mathbb Z)\) is surjective, and so is
its reduction modulo two \cite[Chapter~6]{FarbMargalit}.  Let
\(s\in\operatorname{Sp}_4(\mathbb F_2)\) stabilize the datum and choose a
mapping class \(f\) inducing \(s\).  Put \(\theta=f^*\eta\).
If \(\sigma(\eta)=1\), then the quadratic functions of \(\theta\) and
\(\eta\) have the same reduction modulo two. Their difference is an
even linear form, so there is \(\bar a\in V\) such that
\[
 q_\theta(x)-q_\eta(x)=2(\bar a\cdot\bar x)
 \qquad\text{for }x\in H_1(S;\mathbb Z/4),
\]
where \(\bar x\) denotes reduction modulo two. If \(\bar a=0\),
no correction is needed. Otherwise choose a nonseparating simple
curve \(a\) representing a primitive integral lift of \(\bar a\).
Formula~\textup{(1.7)} in the proof of
\cite[Theorem~1.8]{LekiliPolishchuk} gives
\[
 q_\eta(T_a^2x)-q_\eta(x)
 =2q_\eta([a])([a]\cdot x)\pmod4.
\]
If \(q_\eta([a])\) is odd, \(T_a^2\) corrects the difference.
If it is even, choose a nonseparating simple curve \(c\) with
\(q_\eta([c])\) odd. Then \(\bar a+\bar c\neq0\). Choose a
nonseparating simple curve \(d\) representing a primitive integral
lift of this class. Both \(q_\eta([c])\) and \(q_\eta([d])\) are
odd, and the same formula shows that \(T_d^2T_c^{-2}\) corrects
the difference. These corrections act trivially on
\(H_1(S;\mathbb F_2)\). If \(\sigma(\eta)=0\), stabilization of the
quadratic form \(q_\eta/2\) already gives \(q_\theta=q_\eta\), so no first
correction is needed.

After this correction, \cite[Lemma~1.7]{LekiliPolishchuk} leaves a
difference of the form \(4u\), with \(u\in H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\).  This
second correction may also be chosen in the level-two subgroup.  Indeed,
write the Poincar\'e dual of \(u\) as a sum of primitive nonseparating
classes. By the change-of-coordinates principle
\cite{FarbMargalit}, each such class is represented on
\(\Sigma_{2,1}\) by a curve \(a\) belonging to a genus-one bounding pair
\((a,b)\), and
\[
 (T_aT_b^{-1})^*\eta-\eta=-4a^\vee.
\]
Products of these bounding-pair maps realize every required class \(4u\),
and act trivially on integral homology.  We have therefore constructed a
mapping class \(h\), acting trivially on mod-two homology, such that
\(h^*f^*\eta\simeq\eta\). Thus
\(f\circ h\in\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S)}(\eta)\) realizes \(s\).
The image \(R_\eta\) is the full stabilizer, and the three orders in
\eqref{eq:S21-image-orders} follow.

The orders are distinct.  Hence the conjugacy class of the representation
determines \(\sigma(\eta)\) and, when it is defined, the Arf invariant.
Together with the fixed boundary winding numbers, these are precisely the
invariants in Theorem~\ref{thm:line-field-classification}, and the final assertion
follows.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:framed-stabilizer-presentation}
There are a subgroup \(F_0\leq F\) and an element \(a\in Z(F_0)\) such
that
\begin{align}
 F
 &\simeq F_0\times\mathbb Z\langle T_1\rangle
                  \times\mathbb Z\langle T_2\rangle,
 \label{eq:F0-splitting}\\
 T_3&=aT_1^qT_2^{-q},
 \label{eq:central-element-a}\\
 H_{r,s}
 &\simeq F_0\times\mathbb Z\langle\rho_1\rangle
                  \times\mathbb Z\langle\rho_2\rangle,
 \label{eq:Hrs-splitting}\\
 \rho_3&=a\rho_1^{qr}\rho_2^{-qs}.
 \notag
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
The group \(F\) is also the stabilizer of the absolute homotopy class
of \(\xi\).
Indeed, homotopy classes of vector framings and of line fields are affine
spaces over \(H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\), and projectivization multiplies difference
classes by two.  Since \(H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\) is torsion free, preservation of
\(\mathbb R\xi\) is equivalent to preservation of \(\xi\).

Fix the value of \(\xi\) on the boundary.  Relative framings inducing the
same absolute framing form a torsor over
\begin{equation*}
 L_\partial=
 \ker\bigl(H^1(S,\partial S;\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow H^1(S;\mathbb Z)\bigr)
 \simeq\mathbb Z^3/\mathbb Z(1,1,1).
\end{equation*}
Let \(u_i\) be the image of the \(i\)-th standard basis vector, so that
\(u_1+u_2+u_3=0\).  For \(f\in F\), choose a collar-fixed representative
and define
\[
 \beta(f)=[f^*\xi]-[\xi]\in L_\partial.
\]
A boundary-pointwise mapping class acts trivially on \(L_\partial\), by naturality
of the cohomology sequence of the pair.  The cocycle identity therefore
reduces to \(\beta(fh)=\beta(f)+\beta(h)\), so \(\beta:F\to L_\partial\) is a
homomorphism.

By Lemma~\ref{lem:relative-displacement}, one common sign
\(\varepsilon_0\in\{1,-1\}\) satisfies
\[
 \beta(T_i)=\varepsilon_0\omega_\xi(B_i)u_i.
\]
Write \(\beta(f)=xu_1+yu_2\) and set
\[
 \chi(f)=\varepsilon_0(x,-y)\in\mathbb Z^2.
\]
Using \(u_3=-u_1-u_2\) and the prescribed boundary windings gives
\begin{equation*}
 \chi(T_1)=(1,0),\qquad
 \chi(T_2)=(0,1),\qquad
 \chi(T_3)=(q,-q).
\end{equation*}
Put \(F_0=\ker\chi=\ker\beta\).
This is precisely the subgroup preserving the relative homotopy
class of \(\xi\), as defined in Section~\ref{sec:fibres}.  The twists \(T_1,T_2\) are central in \(F\), and
their images form the standard basis.  This proves
\eqref{eq:F0-splitting}.  The third identity above gives the central
element
\[
 a=T_3T_1^{-q}T_2^q\in F_0,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:central-element-a}.

The three boundary components are fixed because the line-field windings
\((2,-2,-2q)\) are pairwise distinct.  Restriction to the boundary gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary-restriction-sequence}
 1\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow H_{r,s}
 \longrightarrow\mathbb Z/r\mathbb Z\times\mathbb Z/s\mathbb Z\longrightarrow1.
\end{equation}
The rotations \(\rho_1,\rho_2\) give surjectivity.  An element in the
kernel fixes every stop on each boundary circle.  Isotopy extension makes
it the identity on all boundary collars, so it lies in \(F\).

Collar-fixed representatives of \(F\) commute with the \(\rho_i\), and
\begin{equation*}
 \rho_1^r=T_1,\qquad \rho_2^s=T_2,\qquad \rho_3=T_3.
\end{equation*}
These are the only new relations.  Indeed, any relation first projects to
\eqref{eq:boundary-restriction-sequence}, so the exponents of
\(\rho_1,\rho_2\) are divisible by \(r,s\), respectively, after which it
is a relation in \(F\).  Substitution into
\eqref{eq:F0-splitting} and \eqref{eq:central-element-a} proves
\eqref{eq:Hrs-splitting} and the formula for \(\rho_3\).
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:gradable-curves-span}
Let \(S\) have genus at least two, and let \(\xi\) be a vector framing
such that \(\omega_\xi(B)\) is odd on every boundary component.
Then \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\) is generated by classes of nonseparating simple
closed curves \(c\) with \(\omega_\xi(c)=0\). These curves may be chosen
outside any prescribed boundary collars.
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Let \(\eta\) be the line field induced by \(\xi\), so that
\(\omega_\eta=2\omega_\xi\). The quadratic form
\[
 Q_\xi([c])=\omega_\xi(c)+1\pmod2
\]
on \(H_1(S;\mathbb Z/2)\) vanishes on the boundary radical and descends to
the capped surface. See Theorem~\ref{thm:line-field-classification}.
We first show that every integral class \(x\) admitting a nonseparating
simple representative and satisfying \(Q_\xi(\bar x)=1\) has a
zero-winding simple representative. Choose such a representative
\(c\). Its winding is even. Choose a
nonseparating curve \(d\) with \([d]\cdot[c]=1\) and complete it to a
genus-one bounding pair \((d,e)\) containing no original boundary
component. Such a pair exists in genus at least two, as in the proof
of Lemma~\ref{lem:stabilizer-determines-line-field}. Its bounding-pair
map acts trivially on integral homology. Orient \(d\) and \(e\)
homologously. Since they bound a genus-one subsurface containing no
original boundary component,
\[
 \omega_\eta(d)-\omega_\eta(e)=\pm4.
\]
The formulas
\eqref{eq:weighted-bounding-pair},
\eqref{eq:winding-doubling}, and
\eqref{eq:bounding-pair-winding-difference} give
\[
 \Delta\omega_\eta(c)
 =\bigl(\omega_\eta(d)-\omega_\eta(e)\bigr)([d]\cdot[c])=\pm4.
\]
Thus the framing winding changes by \(2\) or \(-2\). Iterating this map
therefore gives a simple representative of \(x\) with winding zero.

A nondegenerate quadratic space of dimension at least four over
\(\mathbb Z/2\) has a symplectic pair on which the quadratic form is one
on both vectors. Lifting a symplectic basis with this first pair,
choose integral handle classes \(a_j,b_j\) with
\(Q_\xi(\bar a_1)=Q_\xi(\bar b_1)=1\). If \(j>1\) and
\(Q_\xi(\bar a_j)=0\), replace \(a_j\), for the purpose of generating
homology, by \(a_j+a_1\). Do the same with \(b_j\) when necessary.
All the resulting primitive nonseparating classes have quadratic
value one and, together with \(a_1,b_1\), generate the handle classes.
For each boundary class \(c_i=[B_i]\), a band sum represents
\(a_1+c_i\) by a nonseparating simple curve, and
\(Q_\xi(\bar a_1+\bar c_i)=1\). Thus \(c_i\) is the difference of
two classes of quadratic value one. These classes generate all of
\(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\), and the first paragraph gives the required
zero-winding representatives. All curves may be chosen outside fixed
boundary collars.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:framed-family-serre-normalization}
The resulting identifications may be chosen as
\[
 D(\mathbb S_{r,s})
 \simeq (N\rtimes F_0)\times
        \mathbb Z\langle\rho_1\rangle\times
        \mathbb Z\langle\rho_2\rangle\times
        \mathbb Z\langle[1]\rangle
\]
so that
\[
 \mathsf S_{r,s}
 =a^{-1}\rho_1^{-(qr+1)}\rho_2^{qs-1}[1]^{e_g},
\]
where \(e_g\in\mathbb Z\) is independent of \(r,s\).
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
The boundary winding numbers of \(\eta\) are
\((2,-2,-2q)\), so the additive factor in
Theorem~\ref{thm:opper-geometrization} is absent. Normalization at \(p\)
splits the grading extension. The displayed group identification now
follows from that theorem and
Lemma~\ref{lem:framed-stabilizer-presentation}. The collar rotations
act trivially on \(N\), since they become isotopic to the identity after
the stops are forgotten. Moreover, \(a\) is a product of boundary
twists. It acts trivially on \(N\) and remains central in
\(N\rtimes F_0\).

Use the geometric section constructed in
\cite[Theorem~7.1]{Opper2025}, together with the graded lifts
normalized at \(p\). By
Proposition~\ref{prop:geometric-serre-functor}, the underlying
ungraded mapping class of the Serre functor is
\((\rho_1\rho_2\rho_3)^{-1}\). Since
\[
 (\rho_1\rho_2\rho_3)^{-1}
 =a^{-1}\rho_1^{-(qr+1)}\rho_2^{qs-1},
\]
the split geometrisation sequence gives
\[
 \mathsf S_{r,s}
 =n_{r,s}a^{-1}\rho_1^{-(qr+1)}
  \rho_2^{qs-1}[1]^{e_{r,s}},
\]
where the local-system factor \(n_{r,s}\in N\) and the shift
exponent \(e_{r,s}\in\mathbb Z\) remain to be determined.

Let \(S^\circ\) be the complement of the interiors of small boundary
collars. The normalized lifts of \(\rho_1,\rho_2\) are the identity
on the grading cover over \(S^\circ\). Their supports avoid both
\(p\) and this connected core. The normalized lift of \(a\) is the
identity as an ungraded map on the core, and hence acts there by a
fixed deck translation \(\kappa_g\). It depends only on \(\eta\), \(a\),
and the normalization at \(p\), and is therefore independent of \(r,s\). Consequently, the normalized lift of \(a^{-1}\) acts on the
grading cover over the core by the deck translation \(-\kappa_g\).
For a nonseparating curve \(c\subset S^\circ\) with
\(\omega_\xi(c)=0\), and hence \(\omega_\eta(c)=0\), let
\(X_{c,\lambda}\) be its rank-one band object with monodromy
\(\lambda\in\mathbf{k}^\times\). Thus \(X_{c,1}\) has trivial local system.
For \(n\in N\) and an oriented closed curve \(c\), write
\[
 n(c)=\langle n,[c]\rangle\in\mathbf{k}^\times.
\]
The local-system factor \(n\) sends \(X_{c,\lambda}\) to
\(X_{c,n(c)\lambda}\). This follows from the description in
\cite[Proposition~10.2]{Opper2025}. For the fixed graded
nonseparating simple closed curve \(c\), the classification of graded
band objects in \cite[Theorem~3.5]{Opper2025} gives
\[
 X_{c,\lambda}[d]\simeq X_{c,1}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \lambda=1\text{ and }d=0.
\]
Indeed, an overall grading shift does not change the rank-one
monodromy parameter and reversing the orientation replaces it by its
inverse, which does not affect comparison with \(1\). Thus the
classification first gives \(\lambda=1\). A non-zero shift cannot
then fix this non-zero object since iterating such a periodicity would give
non-zero self-morphisms in infinitely many degrees, contrary to
properness.

Every band object is fixed by Auslander--Reiten translation
\cite[Proposition~D.1]{OpperPlamondonSchroll}. Therefore
\[
 X_{c,1}\simeq\mathsf S[-1](X_{c,1})
 \simeq X_{c,n_{r,s}(c)}[-\kappa_g+e_{r,s}-1],
\]
and the preceding implication yields
\[
 n_{r,s}(c)=1,
 \qquad -\kappa_g+e_{r,s}-1=0.
\]
The boundary windings of \(\xi\) are all odd, so
Lemma~\ref{lem:gradable-curves-span} shows that these curves generate
\(H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\). Hence
\[
 n_{r,s}=1,\qquad e_{r,s}=1+\kappa_g=:e_g
\]
for every \(r,s\).
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lem:normalized-twisted-collar}
The operator \(R_i\) satisfies \(R_i|_{A_{\mathbb Q}}=I\).
Its induced endpoint action \(\overline R_i\) on
\(H_0(M^\circ\cap B_i;L)\) is a signed cyclic permutation with
\[
 \det(XI-\overline R_i)=X^{m_i}-\nu_i.
\]
Moreover, \(R_i^{m_i}\) is the normalized full boundary-twist action.

If \(\nu_i=1\), then
\[
 (R_i^{m_i}-I)x
 =\varepsilon_\partial c_i\lambda_i(x),
 \qquad (R_i^{m_i}-I)^2=0
\]
for \(x\in K_{\mathbb Q}\),
where \(\varepsilon_\partial\in\{1,-1\}\) is independent of \(i\).

If \(\nu_i=-1\), then \(Q_i\) maps isomorphically to the endpoint
block and
\[
 R_i^{m_i}|_{Q_i}=-I,
 \qquad R_i^{2m_i}=I.
\]
It is the identity on the complementary collar-fixed summand.

In either case, if \(g\geq2\) and \(b\geq2\), the algebraic
multiplicity of \(1\) for \(R_i\) is greater than that of \(-1\).
This distinguishes \(R_i\) from its negative, and
\[
 \operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb Q}(R_i-I)
 =\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb Q}(R_i^{-1}-I)=m_i.
\]
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
The collar rotation is joined to the identity by an isotopy supported
in the collar, during which the stops need not be preserved. The
induced graded lift is the identity on the complement. Any two graded
lifts differ by a global deck transformation, so this normalization is
unique. Following an endpoint once around \(B_i\) multiplies its local
coefficient by \(\nu_i\). This gives the signed cycle and the assertion
about the full power. Absolute cycles may be moved away from the
interior of the collar.

For positive holonomy, a full turn adds the boundary cycle once for
each endpoint coefficient. The added class has no endpoint coefficient,
which proves the square-zero formula and the independence of the collar
sign.

For negative holonomy, choose a core vertex \(v\), a based boundary
loop \(c\), and paths \(u_x\) to the endpoints. Choose local generators
so that \(\partial c=-2v\) and \(\partial u_x=x-v\) in the absolute
chain complex. After quotienting by the endpoint chains, these become
\[
 \partial c=-2v,
 \qquad \partial u_x=-v,
 \qquad q_x=u_x-\frac{c}{2}.
\]
Thus \(q_x\) is a rational relative cycle whose image under the
boundary map of the pair is the chosen generator at \(x\).
The normalized full twist fixes \(c\) and sends \(u_x\) to
\(c-u_x\). It therefore sends \(q_x\) to \(-q_x\). Over
\(\mathbb Q\), the negative-holonomy boundary circle has zero homology.
Mayer--Vietoris splits off its relative collar homology, and the twist
is the identity on the complementary summand. Its square is the
identity over \(\mathbb Q\), and hence over \(\mathbb Z\) because the charge
lattice is torsion free.

We now prove the rank formula. The action on the core has a
\(1\)-eigenspace of dimension at least \(2g+b-2\), while a single
endpoint block contributes at most one \(-1\)-eigenvector. This
selects the stated sign. If \(\nu_i=-1\), the action has finite
order, and its signed cyclic block has no invariant vector. It therefore
contributes rank \(m_i\).

Suppose that \(\nu_i=1\). We claim that the class \(c_i\) is non-zero. For
trivial \(L\) this follows from \(b\geq2\), while for non-trivial
\(L\) it follows from \(H_2(S,\partial S;L)=0\) and the exact
sequence of the pair. The endpoint total \(\lambda_i\) is also
non-zero. For non-trivial \(L\), the map to \(H_0(M^\circ;L)\) is
surjective because \(H_0(S;L)=0\). For trivial \(L\), an arc to
another boundary component has non-zero total at \(B_i\). Hence
\(R_i^{m_i}-I\) has rank one. The non-trivial eigenvalues of the
cyclic block contribute \(m_i-1\) to \(\operatorname{rk}(R_i-I)\).
On the generalized \(1\)-eigenspace, taking a positive power does
not change the rank of the difference from the identity. Indeed, in the
factorization
\[
 R_i^{m_i}-I=(R_i-I)(I+R_i+\cdots+R_i^{m_i-1}),
\]
the second factor is invertible on that subspace. The remaining
contribution is therefore one, so the rank is \(m_i\) in both cases.
Finally, \(R_i^{-1}-I=-R_i^{-1}(R_i-I)\), and inversion does not
change the rank.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lem:twisted-picard-lefschetz}
The following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item If \(c\) is a simple closed curve with
\(\operatorname{hol}_L(c)=1\), choose a primitive local generator along
\(c\). The lift of \(T_c\) fixed away from its twisting annulus satisfies
\[
 (T_c-I)x
 =\varepsilon_{\mathrm{tw}}
   \langle x,[c]_L\rangle_L[c]_L,
 \qquad \varepsilon_{\mathrm{tw}}\in\{1,-1\}.
\]
\item Suppose that \(a,b\) are simple closed curves meeting once, with
\(\operatorname{hol}_L(a)=-1\) and
\(\operatorname{hol}_L(b)=1\). If \(\gamma\) bounds their
one-holed-torus neighbourhood, choose a primitive generator along \(b\)
and the lift of \(T_\gamma\) fixed outside its twisting annulus. Then
\[
 [\gamma]_L=\pm2[b]_L,
 \qquad
 T_\gamma-I=\pm4[b]_L\langle-,[b]_L\rangle_L.
\]

\item If \(c\) is separating and \(\operatorname{hol}_L(c)=-1\), cutting
along \(c\) gives a rational Mayer--Vietoris decomposition. The lift of
\(T_c\) normalized to be the identity on one side acts by \(I\) there
and by \(-I\) on the other, on both absolute and relative twisted homology.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Let \(\widetilde S\to S\) be the double cover defined by \(L\).
Over \(\mathbb Q\), twisted homology identifies with the anti-invariant
part of the homology of \(\widetilde S\). We normalize its intersection
form as one half of the intersection form on the cover. A
positive-holonomy curve has two disjoint lifts, and its twist lifts to
the product of their commuting twists. Restricting the ordinary
Picard--Lefschetz formula to the anti-invariant part proves the first
assertion over \(\mathbb Q\). The same argument applies after lifting
the relative endpoint set.

These formulas are compatible with the integral lattices. Indeed, the
transported-coefficient intersection pairing is integral and a primitive local generator gives an integral
class \([c]_{L_{\mathbb Z}}\). Both sides of the formula therefore preserve the
integral twisted homology lattice. Since that lattice is torsion free,
the rational equality implies the integral equality.

Orient \(a,b\) so that the boundary word of their regular
neighbourhood is \(aba^{-1}b^{-1}\). Write \(a,b\) also for the
corresponding based twisted \(1\)-chains. The Fox derivatives, evaluated
in the sign character of \(L\), give the chain identity
\[
 \gamma=\bigl(1-\operatorname{hol}_L(b)\bigr)a
 +\bigl(\operatorname{hol}_L(a)-1\bigr)b=-2b.
\]
Since \(b\) has positive holonomy, it is a twisted cycle. Passing to
homology gives \([\gamma]_L=-2[b]_L\), and substitution into the first
formula gives the second assertion.

For negative holonomy, the homology of the cutting circle vanishes over
\(\mathbb Q\), so Mayer--Vietoris gives the asserted direct sum.
Crossing the twisting annulus transports a local coefficient once
around that circle. Relative to the normalization on the first side,
the coefficient on the other side is multiplied by \(-1\).
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{lem:twisted-homology-normal-forms}
Assume that \(g\geq2\) and that \(L_{\mathbb Z}\) is non-trivial. The positive
boundary classes form a basis of the radical of the twisted intersection
form on \(A_{\mathbb Q}\).

If every boundary has positive holonomy, there is a geometric handle
basis on which the character of \(L\) is non-trivial only on the first
handle. The remaining handles span a non-degenerate complement of
dimension \(2g-2\).

If some boundary has negative holonomy, there is a geometric handle basis on which \(L\) is
trivial. Its handle core has connected planar complement. The handle
homology is non-degenerate of dimension \(2g\), and the remaining
rational homology is generated by the positive boundary curves and by
planar curves enclosing pairs of negative boundaries.
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Poincar\'e--Lefschetz duality identifies the radical with the image of
boundary homology. Since \(L\) is non-trivial, we have
\(H_2(S,\partial S;L)=0\). Hence, the radical has dimension equal to
the number of positive boundary components.

When every boundary holonomy is positive, the character extends over
the capped surface. A geometric symplectic basis concentrates its
non-zero mod-two character on the first handle. The remaining handle
homology is non-degenerate, and dimensions give the asserted rational
decomposition.

Suppose now that \(B\) has negative holonomy. Pushing \(B\) along a
handle curve \(a\) acts on ordinary homology, up to the push convention,
by
\[
 x\longmapsto x+\langle x,a\rangle[B].
\]
The mod-two character evaluates non-trivially on \([B]\). Therefore pushing along
\(a\) changes the value of the character on the dual handle generator. Since boundary classes lie in the intersection radical, successive pushes
eliminate all handle evaluations while leaving the boundary evaluations
unchanged. In
the resulting planar complement, choose a graph spine with core vertex
\(v\). Positive boundary loops have twisted differential zero, while
negative boundary loops have differential \(-2v\). The kernel is generated by the positive loops and differences
of negative loops. Curves enclosing pairs of negative boundaries realize
these differences modulo the positive boundaries. Gluing the handle
core kills the outer-boundary class and gives the asserted spanning set
in \(H_1(S;L)\).
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- lemma-28: body_md -->
\label{lem:integral-collar-gluing}
The collar classes give a rational decomposition
\[
 K_{\mathbb Q}=A_{\mathbb Q}\oplus
 \bigoplus_{x\in M^\circ}\mathbb Qq_x.
\]
In these coordinates,
\[
 K=\left\{a+\sum_xn_xq_x\ \middle|\
 \begin{array}{c}
  a\in A_{\mathbb Q},\ n_x\in\mathbb Z,\ \sum_xn_x\in2\mathbb Z,\\[2pt]
  a-\frac12\sum_{\nu_i=-1}n_i(c_i-c_{i_0})\in A_{\mathbb Z}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-28 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
The endpoint map first forces every \(n_x\) to be integral. Since
\(H_0(S;L_{\mathbb Z})=\mathbb Z/2\), its image consists exactly of the endpoint
vectors of even total. Thus \(\sum_xn_x\in2\mathbb Z\). Expanding the
collar coordinates gives
\[
 a+\sum_xn_xq_x
 =a-\frac12\sum_{\nu_i=-1}n_i(c_i-c_{i_0})
  +\sum_xn_xu_x-\frac12\left(\sum_xn_x\right)c_{i_0}.
\]
The last two terms are now integral. The whole expression is therefore
integral if and only if the first term belongs to \(A_{\mathbb Z}\).
Conversely, these conditions produce an integral relative cycle, proving
the formula.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- lemma-29: body_md -->
\label{lem:separating-twisted-scalar-commutant}
Assume that \(S\) has genus at least two and that \(L\) is non-trivial.
For every curve
\(\gamma\) bounding a one-holed torus in the interior of \(S\), give
\(T_\gamma\) the lift fixed away from its twisting annulus. The
commutant of these actions in
\(\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb Q}(A_{\mathbb Q})\) is
\(\mathbb Q I_{A_{\mathbb Q}}\).
<!-- end lemma-29 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
First consider a positive-holonomy nonseparating simple curve \(b\).
It has a negative-holonomy simple partner \(a\) meeting it once.
Indeed, choose any once-intersecting partner. If both curves have
positive holonomy, their one-holed torus has trivial local system, so
its connected complement has non-trivial local system. This complement
has a negative-holonomy simple closed curve. Simple handle and boundary
curves generate its first homology, and the character is
non-trivial on at least one such generator. Join that curve to the
chosen partner by an embedded band whose interior avoids \(b\).
The resulting simple curve still meets \(b\) once, while its holonomy
is the product of the two original holonomies and is therefore negative.
The band and the curves may all be chosen in the interior of \(S\).

The boundary \(\gamma\) of a regular neighbourhood of \(a\cup b\)
belongs to the family in the statement. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-picard-lefschetz},
\[
 [\gamma]_L=\pm2[b]_L,\qquad
 T_\gamma-I=\pm4N_b,\qquad
 N_b(x)=\langle x,[b]_L\rangle_L[b]_L.
\]
Thus an endomorphism commuting with the stated twists commutes with
\(N_b\) for every positive-holonomy nonseparating curve \(b\).
Whenever \(N_b\neq0\), it preserves
\(\operatorname{im}N_b=\mathbb Q[b]_L\).

Choose the normal form in
Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-homology-normal-forms}. It gives a
non-degenerate handle subspace \(W\) of dimension \(2g-2\) when all
boundary holonomies are positive, and of dimension \(2g\) otherwise.
Put \(V_{\mathrm{comp}}=W^\perp\), so that
\(
 A_{\mathbb Q}=W\oplus V_{\mathrm{comp}}\).
The complementary subspace is spanned by positive boundary classes and,
when needed, by positive-holonomy curves enclosing pairs of negative
boundaries. These curves may be chosen disjoint from the handle core.

For a twisted homology class \(v\), we also write
\(N_v(x)=\langle x,v\rangle_Lv\). Let \(U\) commute with the stated
twists, and choose a geometric symplectic basis \(e_1,f_1,\ldots,e_h,f_h\) of \(W\), where
\(h=\tfrac12\dim W\geq1\). The associated handle curves have positive
holonomy. Commutation with \(N_{e_i}\) and \(N_{f_i}\) makes the
lines \(\mathbb Qe_i\) and \(\mathbb Qf_i\) invariant. Applying
\(UN_{e_i}=N_{e_i}U\) to \(f_i\) shows that the two eigenvalues agree.
Write them as \(a_i\). Moreover,
\[
 V_{\mathrm{comp}}
 =\bigcap_{i=1}^h\bigl(\ker N_{e_i}\cap\ker N_{f_i}\bigr),
\]
so this subspace is also \(U\)-invariant. For \(i>1\), a band sum of
handle curves represents \(e_1+e_i\), after compatible choices of local
generators. Its transvection is non-zero, and invariance of
\(\mathbb Q(e_1+e_i)\) gives \(a_i=a_1\). Hence
\(U|_W=\lambda I_W\), with \(\lambda=a_1\). This also covers
\(h=1\), when no comparison between different handles is required.

For each generator \(u\) of \(V_{\mathrm{comp}}\) described above,
band-sum its curve with the handle curve representing \(e_1\). The
result is positive-holonomy and nonseparating, with twisted class
\(e_1\pm u\). Since its pairing with \(f_1\) is non-zero, its
transvection is non-zero. Thus \(U\) preserves
\(\mathbb Q(e_1\pm u)\). Comparing the \(W\)-coordinate with
\(Ue_1=\lambda e_1\), and using the invariance of
\(V_{\mathrm{comp}}\), gives \(Uu=\lambda u\). These generators span
\(V_{\mathrm{comp}}\), so \(U=\lambda I_{A_{\mathbb Q}}\). Conversely,
scalar endomorphisms commute with every action, proving the assertion.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- lemma-30: body_md -->
\label{lem:absolute-intertwiner-scalar}
Let \(\mathbb S=(S,M,\eta)\) and \(\mathbb S'=(S',M',\eta')\) have genus
at least two and at least two boundary components. Let
\((\Phi,\Theta):\mathfrak R(\mathbb S)\to\mathfrak R(\mathbb S')\) be
an isomorphism, and let \(f\) and \(\phi\) be supplied by
Corollary~\ref{cor:geometric-reduction}. After
choosing an integral identification of the orientation local systems,
\[
 \Theta|_{H_1(S;L_{\mathbb Z})}=\varepsilon f_*,
 \qquad \varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-30 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
By Proposition~\ref{prop:represented-boundary-data}, \(\Theta\)
carries the saturated absolute lattice on the source to the one on the
target. Transport the target by \(f\) and write \(V\) for the restriction
of the resulting intertwiner to \(A_{\mathbb Q}\).

Suppose first that \(L\) is trivial. Graded equivariance gives a character
\(e:H_\eta\to\{1,-1\}\) such that
\(
 Vy_*=e(y)y_*V\). The kernel of \(e\) has finite index. Choose a vector framing
inducing \(\eta\) and fix its boundary values. The relative framing
stabilizer is contained in the stabilizer of the absolute framing, hence in \(H_\eta\). By
\cite[Theorem~C]{CalderonSalterRelative}, for \(g\geq2\), its
action on \(H_1(S,\partial S;\mathbb Z)\) has finite-index image in the pure
homological automorphism group. This image is the kernel of a crossed
homomorphism with finite target. Poincar\'e--Lefschetz duality
transfers this statement to absolute homology. Let \(R_\partial\)
be the boundary radical and choose a symplectic complement \(W\).
In the splitting \(A_{\mathbb Q}=R_\partial\oplus W\), the resulting
Zariski closure is the pure parabolic group
\[
 \left\{\begin{pmatrix}I&B\\0&S\end{pmatrix}:
 S\in\operatorname{Sp}(W),\quad
 B\in\operatorname{Hom}(W,R_\partial)\right\}.
\]
This algebraic group is connected. Therefore, intersecting the relative framing
stabilizer with \(\ker e\) does not change its Zariski closure, so \(V\) commutes with the displayed group.
The commutant of its symplectic subgroup is block diagonal and scalar
on \(W\). Commutation with every \(B\) makes it scalar on the boundary
radical with the same scalar. Hence \(V=aI\). Integrality gives
\(a=\pm1\). The equivariance equation then forces \(e(y)=1\) for every
\(y\).

Now suppose that \(L\) is non-trivial. For every one-holed-torus
separating curve \(\gamma\), let \(G_\gamma\) be the local-system action
normalized outside its twisting annulus. Corollary~\ref{cor:geometric-reduction}
and Lemma~\ref{lem:geometrization-charge-action} give
\[
 VG_\gamma V^{-1}=e_\gamma G_\gamma,
 \qquad e_\gamma\in\{1,-1\}.
\]
The operator \(G_\gamma\) is unipotent, while \(-G_\gamma\) has only
the eigenvalue \(-1\). Thus \(e_\gamma=1\). Lemma~\ref{lem:separating-twisted-scalar-commutant}
shows that \(V\) is scalar. Preservation of the integral lattice again
gives \(V=\pm I\). Restoring \(f_*\) proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- lemma-31: body_md -->
\label{lem:integral-negative-boundary-matching}
The following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For distinct negative boundaries \(B_i,B_j\),
\[
 [B_i]+[B_j]=[B_{\pi(i)}]+[B_{\pi(j)}]
 \quad\text{in }H_1(S;\mathbb F_2).
\]
If there are at least four negative boundaries, every negative block
is fixed unless \(b=4\) and every boundary is negative. In that case
\(\pi\) belongs to the Klein four group.
\item If there are exactly two negative boundaries and at least two
positive boundaries, then both negative blocks are fixed.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-31 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
For a negative boundary, let \(Q_i\subset K_{\mathbb Q}\) be its
canonical sum of non-trivial primary spaces. A local generator at an
endpoint \(x\) gives a collar class \(q_x\), the negative half of the
twisted boundary loop based at \(x\), with endpoint coefficient \(x\).
Thus \(Q_i\) maps isomorphically to its endpoint block.
Projection of \(K\) to \(Q_i\) gives a lattice
\(\Lambda_i\cong\mathbb Z^{m_i}\). Its intersection
\(\Lambda_i^{\mathrm{ev}}=Q_i\cap K\) consists of the vectors with even
total coefficient. Hence
\(\Lambda_i/\Lambda_i^{\mathrm{ev}}\cong\mathbb F_2\).
The assertion follows directly from the relative chain complex of
the negative-holonomy circle. Boundary loops have endpoint coefficient
\(\pm2\), while paths between endpoints give signed differences.
Projection realizes an odd coordinate because another boundary can
supply the second odd endpoint. Both lattices are intrinsic.

For two negative blocks choose \(v_i\in\Lambda_i\) and
\(v_j\in\Lambda_j\) with odd parity. There is an integral
class \(x\in K\) with these endpoint coordinates and no others.
The image of the endpoint map is the even-total lattice, as
\(H_0(S;L_{\mathbb Z})=\mathbb Z/2\). Write
\[
 a=2x-2v_i-2v_j\in A_{\mathbb Z}.
\]
Its class \(\delta_{ij}\in A_{\mathbb Z}/2A_{\mathbb Z}\) is
independent of all choices. For another choice
\(x',v_i',v_j'\), the differences \(v_i'-v_i\) and
\(v_j'-v_j\) lie in \(\Lambda_i^{\mathrm{ev}}\) and
\(\Lambda_j^{\mathrm{ev}}\). They are integral, and
cancellation of the endpoint coordinates gives
\[
 x'-x-(v_i'-v_i)-(v_j'-v_j)\in A_{\mathbb Z}.
\]
The two resulting absolute classes therefore differ by an element of
\(2A_{\mathbb Z}\). An integral intertwiner carries \(\delta_{ij}\) to the
corresponding class for the permuted blocks.

Reduction of the twisted chain complex modulo two gives the ordinary
chain complex. Since \(H_0(S;L_{\mathbb Z})\simeq\mathbb Z/2\), the universal
coefficient sequence is
\[
 0\longrightarrow A_{\mathbb Z}/2A_{\mathbb Z}
 \longrightarrow H_1(S;\mathbb F_2)
 \longrightarrow
 \operatorname{Tor}_1^{\mathbb Z}(H_0(S;L_{\mathbb Z}),\mathbb F_2)
 \longrightarrow0.
\]
The class of \(2x\) reduces to zero, while \(2v_i\) and \(2v_j\) reduce
to the two ordinary boundary loops.
Consequently \(\delta_{ij}\)
maps to \([B_i]+[B_j]\). The injection in the displayed universal
coefficient sequence is essential here. In particular, one must not
identify \(A_{\mathbb Z}/2A_{\mathbb Z}\) with all of \(H_1(S;\mathbb F_2)\), since the
final Tor term is non-zero. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:absolute-intertwiner-scalar}, the absolute intertwiner
is \(\pm f_*\). Naturality of the displayed injection therefore
identifies the two boundary sums. The common sign disappears modulo
two, giving the asserted identity.

Over \(\mathbb F_2\), the boundary classes have the single relation
\(\sum_i[B_i]=0\). If \(b\ne4\) and there
are at least four negative boundaries, equality of two sums of
distinct pairs forces equality of the unordered pairs. A permutation
of at least three elements fixing every unordered pair is the
identity.

For \(b=4\), a pair may instead be replaced by its
complement. The permutations preserving the three partitions into
complementary pairs form the Klein four group. This proves
\textup{(i)}.

By Proposition~\ref{prop:geometric-boundary-matching}, the positive
boundaries have matching stop numbers. Identify their stop sets by
isotopies in boundary collars. Multiplying the intertwiner by a common
sign, we may assume
\(
 \Theta|_{A_{\mathbb Q}}=I\). Write \(b_+\) for the number of positive boundary components.
Write \(B^-_1,B^-_2\) for the negative boundaries and
\(B^+_1,\ldots,B^+_{b_+}\) for the positive boundaries. Denote the
positive boundary classes by \(d_1,\ldots,d_{b_+}\), and set
\[
 R=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}\{d_1,\ldots,d_{b_+}\}.
\]
The classes \(d_i\) are independent by
Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-homology-normal-forms}.

Set \(F=\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Mod}(S,\partial S)}(\eta)\).
Proposition~\ref{prop:represented-boundary-data} identifies the
boundary-pointwise stabilizers. After the geometric transport, the
induced isomorphism on their common underlying group \(F\) has the form
\[
 y\longmapsto yz(y),
 \qquad z(y)=\prod_B T_B^{n_B(y)}.
\]
Here \(B\) ranges over the boundary components and
\(n_B(y)\in\mathbb Z\).
Indeed, the two maps have the same image after the stops are forgotten,
while their difference on \(F\) lies in the central lattice generated
by the full boundary twists.
The full twists, with the complement-fixed normalization of
Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-twisted-collar}, act trivially on absolute
twisted homology. Since \(\Theta|_{A_{\mathbb Q}}=I\), the equivariance equation has no
additional common sign when the same local-system lift of \(y\) is
used on both sides.

We now prove that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-negative-fixed-totals}
 \lambda_i\Theta=\lambda_i
 \qquad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq b_+,
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda_i\) is the endpoint total at the \(i\)-th positive
boundary \(B_i^+\). Let \(\beta\) be a
positive-holonomy nonseparating curve with nonradical twisted class.
Choose a negative-holonomy partner meeting it once, as in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:separating-twisted-scalar-commutant}. The boundary
\(\gamma\) of their regular neighbourhood is separating. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-picard-lefschetz}, its twist acts on relative
homology as
\[
 I+N_\beta,
 \qquad N_\beta=a_\beta[\beta]\otimes\lambda_\beta,
 \qquad a_\beta\neq0,
\]
where \(\lambda_\beta\) is the twisted intersection covector. This action
is the identity on the endpoint quotient.

The image of \(T_\gamma\) under the represented isomorphism must also
act identically on that quotient. The negative-boundary exponents in
\(z(T_\gamma)\) are therefore even, and their relative actions are
trivial by Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-twisted-collar}. The remaining
central correction has the form
\[
 I+D,
 \qquad
 D=\varepsilon_\partial\sum_i n_i d_i\otimes\lambda_i,\qquad
 n_i\in\mathbb Z.
\]
Since \(N_\beta D=DN_\beta=0\) and \(\Theta N_\beta=N_\beta\), expansion
of the equivariance equation gives
\[
 N_\beta-N_\beta\Theta=D\Theta.
\]
The left-hand side has image in \(\mathbb Q[\beta]\), while the right-hand
side has image in \(R\). These spaces intersect trivially because
\([\beta]\) is nonradical. Hence, we have
\(\lambda_\beta\Theta=\lambda_\beta\).

Band-sum \(\beta\) with \(B_i^+\). The resulting
curve is positive-holonomy and nonseparating, with class
\([\beta]\pm d_i\). The same argument applies to it. With compatible choices of signs, the
twisted intersection pairing gives
\[
 \lambda_{\beta\pm d_i}=\lambda_\beta\pm\lambda_i.
\] Subtracting the invariant
covectors proves \eqref{eq:two-negative-fixed-totals}.

Let \(M_+^\circ\) be the set of endpoints on positive boundaries and put
\[
 C_-=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}\{r_i:\nu_i=-1\}
 \subset C_{\mathbb Q}.
\]
The collar
decomposition and Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-twisted-collar} identify
\[
 K_{+,\mathbb Q}
 =K_{\mathbb Q}^{C\cap C_-}
 =H_1(S,M_+^\circ;L).
\]
The action on this fixed subspace factors through
\(C/(C\cap C_-)\). Let \(V_0\) be the simultaneous generalized
trivial-character subspace for this quotient action. This definition
is intrinsic and does not require a choice of lifts of the positive
quotient axes to \(C\). In particular, the represented intertwiner
carries \(V_0\) to its target counterpart. Collar rotations commute
with the \(F\)-action, so \(V_0\) is an \(F\)-submodule. It fits into
\[
 0\longrightarrow A_{\mathbb Q}
 \longrightarrow V_0
 \xrightarrow{(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_{b_+})}
 \mathbb Q^{b_+}\longrightarrow0.
\]
The positive endpoint sets have already been identified. The inclusion
of pairs therefore identifies both copies of \(K_{+,\mathbb Q}\) with
the same \(H_1(S,M_+^\circ;L)\), including their uncorrected \(F\)-actions
and the subspace \(V_0\). The actual actions differ only by the central
boundary corrections above. Together with
\(\Theta|_{A_{\mathbb Q}}=I\), equation
\eqref{eq:two-negative-fixed-totals} now gives
\[
 \Theta|_{V_0}=I+\Delta,
 \qquad \Delta|_{A_{\mathbb Q}}=0,
 \qquad \Delta(V_0)\subseteq A_{\mathbb Q}.
\]

The normal form in
Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-homology-normal-forms} gives a
non-degenerate \(2g\)-dimensional handle complement to \(R\). The
restrictions to \(A_{\mathbb Q}\) of the intersection covectors
of its positive-holonomy handle curves have common annihilator \(R\).
Their invariance under \(\Theta\) therefore
implies \(\Delta(V_0)\subseteq R\). Write
\[
 \Delta=\sum_{i,j}\Delta_{ij}d_i\otimes\lambda_j.
\]

For \(y\in F\), let \(\mu_i(y)\in\{1,-1\}\) be its coefficient sign
at the \(i\)-th positive boundary. Naturality gives
\[
 y_*d_i=\mu_i(y)d_i,
 \qquad \lambda_i y_*=\mu_i(y)\lambda_i.
\]
On \(V_0\), a central correction has the form \(I+D_y\), with
\(D_y\) in the span of the diagonal operators
\(d_i\otimes\lambda_i\). Since both \(D_y\) and \(\Delta\) vanish on
absolute homology, the equivariance equation gives
\[
 \Delta y_*-y_*\Delta=y_*D_y.
\]
Its off-diagonal coefficients satisfy
\[
 \bigl(\mu_j(y)-\mu_i(y)\bigr)\Delta_{ij}=0
 \qquad\text{ for }i\neq j.
\]

For each \(i\), choose a separating curve enclosing \(B^-_1\) and
\(B_i^+\), and no other boundary component. Its
holonomy is negative. Normalize the local-system lift of its twist
to be the identity on the side containing the other positive boundaries.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:twisted-picard-lefschetz}, this gives
\(\mu_i=-1\) and \(\mu_j=1\) for \(j\neq i\). Changing the lift
changes all signs at once and does not affect the displayed equation.

For a fixed ordered pair \(i\ne j\), this choice gives
\(\mu_j-\mu_i=2\), up to a common sign. Over \(\mathbb Q\), the coefficient equation therefore forces \(\Delta_{ij}=0\).
Varying \(i\) eliminates every off-diagonal coefficient. Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-negative-diagonal-correction}
 \Theta|_{V_0}=I+\sum_i a_i d_i\otimes\lambda_i.
\end{equation}

We now retain the integral information. Use the two-negative normal
form in \eqref{eq:two-negative-absolute-normal-form} and write
\[
 c_2-c_1=d_1+\cdots+d_{b_+}=:d,
 \qquad
 A_{\mathbb Z}=H_{\mathbb Z}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{b_+}\mathbb Z d_i.
\]
Choose the collar coordinates \(q_x\) from
Lemma~\ref{lem:integral-collar-gluing} with
\(B^-_1\) as the base boundary. 
An expression with absolute part
\(a\in A_{\mathbb Q}\) and endpoint coefficients \(n_x\) is integral
if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-negative-integral-lattice}
 n_x\in\mathbb Z,
 \qquad \sum_xn_x\in2\mathbb Z,
 \qquad a-\frac{n^-_2}{2}d\in A_{\mathbb Z},
 \qquad n^-_2=\sum_{x\in M^\circ\cap B^-_2}n_x.
\end{equation}

Let \(Q_k\) be the canonical negative block at \(B^-_k\). Projection of
\(K\) to \(Q_k\) gives
\[
 \Lambda_k=\bigoplus_{x\in M^\circ\cap B^-_k}\mathbb Z q_x,
 \qquad
 Q_k\cap K=
 \left\{\sum_{x\in M^\circ\cap B^-_k}n_xq_x\ \middle|\
             \sum_{x\in M^\circ\cap B^-_k}n_x\in2\mathbb Z\right\}.
\]
These are the intrinsic lattices from the first part of the proof. If \(\Theta\) exchanges
\(Q_1\) and \(Q_2\), the image of any \(q_x\) with
\(x\in M^\circ\cap B^-_1\)
has integral coordinates and odd total coefficient in \(Q_2\).

It remains to control the extra endpoints on a positive boundary. Let
\(W_j\) be the sum of the non-trivial primary spaces of its rotation.
For two endpoints \(y,y'\) on \(B_j^+\), set
\[
 U_j=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}
 \{d_j,\ q_y-q_{y'}\mid y,y'\in M^\circ\cap B_j^+\}.
\]
The full twist is trivial on \(U_j\), so the one-step rotation
has finite order there and is semisimple. Its endpoint quotient
is the zero-total cyclic module, which has no invariant vector.
Hence its invariant subspace is exactly \(\mathbb Qd_j\).
Consequently,
\(
 U_j=\mathbb Qd_j\oplus W_j\). In particular, \(W_j\) has no absolute boundary coordinate along
\(d_i\) for \(i\neq j\). Decompose
\(q_y=v_y+w_y\), with \(v_y\in V_0\) and \(w_y\in W_j\). The vector
\(v_y\) has endpoint-total vector equal to the \(j\)-th standard
basis vector. The positive block at \(B_j^+\) has already been matched,
so \(\Theta(W_j)=W_j\). Equation
\eqref{eq:two-negative-diagonal-correction} now gives
\(
 \Theta q_y-q_y\in U_j\). Thus \(\Theta q_y\) has total endpoint coefficient one on \(B_j^+\), no
endpoint coefficients on the other positive boundaries, and no
absolute coordinate along \(d_i\) for \(i\neq j\).
