Choose \(x\in M^\circ\cap B^-_1\) and an endpoint \(y\) on \(B_j^+\).
The vector
\(q_x+q_y\) is integral by
\eqref{eq:two-negative-integral-lattice}. If the negative blocks were
exchanged, its image would have odd total coefficient on \(B^-_2\).
The same integral lattice condition would force its absolute
coefficient along every \(d_i\) to be \(1/2\) modulo \(\mathbb Z\). For
\(i\neq j\), the preceding arguments show that this coefficient is
zero. Such an \(i\) exists because \(b_+\geq2\). This contradiction
proves that the negative blocks cannot be exchanged, completing
\textup{(ii)}.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- lemma-32: body_md -->
\label{lem:normalized-representation-extension}
Suppose that \(\phi:H_\eta\to H_{\eta'}\) is an isomorphism and that
\(\Theta:K(\mathbb S)\to K(\mathbb S')\) intertwines the normalized
stabilizer representations. If there is an isomorphism
\(h:H_1(S;\mathbb Z)\to H_1(S';\mathbb Z)\) satisfying
\(hy_*=\phi(y)_*h\) for every \(y\in H_\eta\), then \((\phi,\Theta)\)
extends to a shift-preserving isomorphism of represented invariants.
<!-- end lemma-32 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
The non-zero windings eliminate the additive factors in the
geometrisation kernel of Theorem~\ref{thm:opper-geometrization}.
Normalization at the fixed stop splits the grading extension.
Choose a splitting of the geometrisation sequence and restrict it
to the normalized copy of \(H_\eta\) in the graded mapping class
group. Together with the actual central shift \([1]\), this gives
the displayed decomposition of \(D(\mathbb S)\). We also make the same
choices for \(\mathbb S'\).
Define
\[
 h^\vee:H^1(S;\mathbf{k}^\times)\longrightarrow H^1(S';\mathbf{k}^\times),
 \qquad h^\vee(\chi)=\chi\circ h^{-1}.
\]
The assumed ordinary homological equivariance makes \(h^\vee\)
equivariant for \(\phi\). It therefore extends \(\phi\) to the
semidirect products in the displayed decomposition. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:geometrization-charge-action}, the local-system torus
acts trivially on the charge lattices, while the shift acts by \(-I\). Sending
\((\chi,y,n)\) to \((h^\vee(\chi),\phi(y),n)\) and using \(\Theta\)
on the charge lattice gives the required represented isomorphism.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- lemma-33: body_md -->
\label{lem:exceptional-representation-calculations}
In each of the following cases, the formula for \(\phi\) defines an
isomorphism of the stopped line-field stabilizers and admits an integral
intertwiner of the normalized charge representations:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item the two-boundary construction of
Example~\ref{ex:two-boundary-represented-failure};
\item the three-boundary construction of
Example~\ref{ex:three-boundary-represented-failure};
\item the four-boundary construction of
Example~\ref{ex:four-boundary-represented-failure}.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, the two represented invariants in each example are
isomorphic.
<!-- end lemma-33 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
We retain the notation of the respective examples. In each case the
source \(\mathbb S\) and target \(\mathbb S'\) have the same underlying
surface and orientation local system, and we use common notation for
absolute homology. With the line fields, stop counts, characters, and
explicit formulas specified there, we verify the root relations and
integral equivariance separately in each case.

In all three cases, the central correction on \(F\) is invertible.
Its defining characters vanish on the correcting central elements,
so negating the correction exponents gives the inverse. We also check
the root relations and the induced maps on the finite quotients by \(F\).

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Proof of \textup{(i):} two boundary components.}
The root relation is preserved because
\[
 \phi(\rho_2)^m=T_1^{-q^2}T_2^{2m}=\alpha(T_2).
\]
On the quotient by \(F\), the map is multiplication by
\(-q^2\equiv-1\pmod m\), so it is an isomorphism. It induces the
identity after the boundary twists are forgotten and hence on ordinary
homology.

The orientation local system is positive on the handles and negative
on both boundaries. Equation~\eqref{eq:two-negative-absolute-normal-form}
then shows that the two negative boundary chains agree.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:integral-collar-gluing}, after exchanging the source
and target boundary blocks, both charge lattices have the model
\[
 K=A_{\mathbb Z}\oplus
 \left\{\sum_x n_xq_x:n_x\in\mathbb Z,\quad
                         \sum_x n_x\equiv0\pmod2\right\}.
\]
Normalize source lifts at the unique stop on \(B_1\) and target lifts
at the unique stop on \(B_2\). For \(y\in F\), relative displacement gives the signs
\[
 \begin{array}{c|cc}
  &B_1&B_2\\\hline
  \text{source}&1&(-1)^{\beta(y)}\\
  \text{target}&(-1)^{\beta(y)}&1.
 \end{array}
\]
The lift of \(k\) which is the identity off its boundary collars changes
both endpoint signs and fixes absolute homology. After normalization it
acts by \(-I\) on absolute homology and trivially on the endpoint
blocks. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:relative-displacement}, changing the normalization of \(y\) from \(B_1\) to \(B_2\)
multiplies its absolute action by \((-1)^{\beta(y)}\). The factor \(k^{\beta(y)}\)
contributes the same sign and cancels this discrepancy. Thus the target
action of \(\alpha(y)\) agrees with the source action of \(y\) on
absolute homology, while the two endpoint sign characters agree after
exchanging the blocks. Choose cyclic bases
\(e_0,\ldots,e_{m-1}\) and \(e'_0,\ldots,e'_{m-1}\) for the non-trivial
source and target root blocks, and denote the corresponding signed cycles
by \(R\) and \(R'\). Then \(R^m=(R')^m=-I\). Since
\(q^2\equiv1\pmod{2m}\), its target image acts as \((R')^{-1}\).
The signed reversal
\[
 e_0\longmapsto e'_0,
 \qquad e_j\longmapsto-e'_{m-j}\quad\text{ for }1\leq j<m
\]
intertwines these cycles. Exchanging the two endpoint blocks by this
map, and taking the identity on \(A_{\mathbb Z}\), preserves the even-total
lattice. Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-representation-extension} gives the
required isomorphism of represented invariants.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Proof of \textup{(ii):} three boundary components.}
Here
\[
 \phi(\rho_2)^m=T_1^{-q}T_2^{2m}=\alpha(T_2),
 \qquad -q\equiv1\pmod m.
\]
The map is therefore an isomorphism. Its matrix on the first two
rotation directions has determinant
\(2m(1-q)+q^2=1\), and it fixes the third direction.

Let \(c_1,c_2\) be the two negative boundary chains and put
\(d=c_2-c_1=[B_3]_L\), up to the common collar convention. Write
\[
 A_{\mathbb Z}=H_{\mathbb Z}\oplus\mathbb Z d,
 \qquad
 K_{\mathbb Q}=H_{\mathbb Q}\oplus\mathbb Qd
       \oplus\mathbb Qz\oplus E_1\oplus E_2,
\]
for the source, where \(H_{\mathbb Q}=H_{\mathbb Z}\otimes\mathbb Q\). The
target has the analogous decomposition with \(E_1\) and \(E_2\)
exchanged. Choose the source collar coordinates
\(q_x\) from Lemma~\ref{lem:integral-collar-gluing} with \(B_1\) as
the base boundary. Here
\[
 E_i=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Q}
 \{q_x:x\in M^\circ\cap B_i\}
 \quad\text{ for }i=1,2,
\]
and \(x_3\) is the unique endpoint on \(B_3\), with collar coordinate
\(z=q_{x_3}\).
An element of \(K_{\mathbb Q}\) has a unique expression
\[
 x_H+ad+n_3z+
 \sum_{x\in (M^\circ\cap B_1)\sqcup(M^\circ\cap B_2)}n_xq_x,
 \qquad
 x_H\in H_{\mathbb Q},\quad a,n_3,n_x\in\mathbb Q.
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:integral-collar-gluing} shows that this element is
integral exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{eq:three-boundary-integral-conditions}
 \begin{gathered}
 x_H\in H_{\mathbb Z},\qquad n_3,n_x\in\mathbb Z,\qquad
 n_1+n_2+n_3\equiv0\pmod2,\\
 a-\frac{n_2}{2}\in\mathbb Z,
 \qquad n_i=\sum_{x\in M^\circ\cap B_i}n_x\quad\text{ for }i=1,2.
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Normalize both sides at the endpoint on \(B_3\). The lift of
\(k=T_1^{-1}T_2\) is \(-I\) on \(E_1\oplus E_2\) and the identity on
\(
 U=H_{\mathbb Q}\oplus\mathbb Qd\oplus\mathbb Qz\), which is the image of
\(H_1(S,\{x_3\};L)\). The two inclusions are \(F\)-equivariant for
the common normalization, so the source and target actions agree on
\(U\). For \(y\in F\), let \(\delta_i(y)\) be the scalar by which this
normalized lift acts on \(E_i\). Relative displacement gives
\(\delta_1(y)\delta_2(y)=(-1)^{h(y)}\). Since \(q\) is odd, the factor
\(k^{qh(y)}\) exchanges the two endpoint sign characters. The full
twist at \(B_3\) has the form \(I+N_3\), where
\[
 N_3(z)=\varepsilon_\partial d,
 \qquad N_3(H_{\mathbb Q}\oplus\mathbb Qd)=0.
\]
It commutes with the stabilizer action. Since
\(\varepsilon_\partial^2=1\), the operator
\(I+\varepsilon_\partial N_3/2\) fixes
\(H_{\mathbb Q}\oplus\mathbb Qd\) and sends \(z\) to \(z+d/2\).
Composing it with the exchange of \(E_1\) and \(E_2\) therefore
intertwines \(F\) with \(\alpha(F)\). On the coordinates in
\eqref{eq:three-boundary-integral-conditions}, it sends
\[
 (n_1,n_2,n_3,a)\longmapsto(n_2,n_1,n_3,a+n_3/2).
\]
The even-total condition shows that this map and its inverse are
integral. Let \(E'_1\) be the target endpoint block at \(B_1\). The root
actions on \(E_2\) and \(E'_1\) are signed cycles \(R,R'\) with
\(R^m=(R')^m=-I\), and
\[
 \varrho_{\mathbb S'}\bigl(\phi(\rho_2)\bigr)|_{E'_1}
 =(R')^{-q}=R'.
\]
Thus the root actions are also intertwined. The coarse homological
action is unchanged, so
Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-representation-extension} applies.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Proof of \textup{(iii):} four boundary components.}
The root relations follow from \(1+q_m=2m\) and \(1-q_n=-2n\).
The induced maps on the cyclic quotients are invertible because
\[
 q_m^2\equiv1\pmod{2m},
 \qquad q_n^2\equiv1\pmod{2n}.
\]
Thus \(\phi\) is an isomorphism and induces the identity on the
common image of the two stabilizers in \(\operatorname{Mod}(S)\).

Choose a core vertex \(v\) and negative boundary chains with
\[
 c_4=c_1-c_2+c_3,
 \qquad \partial c_i=-2v.
\]
For
\(x\in M^\circ\cap B_i\), write \(i(x)=i\) and choose the collar
coordinate \(q_x\) from Lemma~\ref{lem:integral-collar-gluing}
with \(B_1\) as the base boundary. For endpoint coordinates
\(\mathbf n=(n_x)\), put
\[
 \Lambda_{\mathrm{ev}}=
 \left\{\mathbf n\in\mathbb Z^{M^\circ}:\sum_xn_x\equiv0\pmod2\right\},
 \qquad
 \Gamma(\mathbf n)=\frac12\sum_xn_x(c_{i(x)}-c_1).
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:integral-collar-gluing} identifies the charge lattice as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:four-negative-integral-gluing}
 K=\left\{a+\sum_xn_xq_x:\begin{array}{l}
 a\in A_{\mathbb Q},\quad \mathbf n\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
 a-\Gamma(\mathbf n)\in A_{\mathbb Z}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Let \(\Gamma'\) be the corresponding correction term for the target,
again based at \(B_1\). Its charge lattice has the same form with
\(\Gamma\) replaced by \(\Gamma'\).
For \(\pi=(12)(34)\), let \(\bar c_i\) denote the class represented by
the reduction of \(c_i\) in \(H_1(S;\mathbb F_2)\). Then
\[
 \bar c_{\pi(i)}-\bar c_i=\bar c_1+\bar c_2
 \qquad\text{for every }i.
\]
Let \(\Pi\) be any signed source-to-target endpoint permutation inducing
\(\pi\). Since
vectors in \(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ev}}\) have even total, we have
\[
 2\bigl(\Gamma'(\Pi\mathbf n)-\Gamma(\mathbf n)\bigr)
 \equiv
 \left(\sum_x n_x\right)(\bar c_1+\bar c_2)=0\pmod2.
\]
The integral chain on the left is a cycle which vanishes in
\(H_1(S;\mathbb F_2)\). The universal coefficient injection
\[
 A_{\mathbb Z}/2A_{\mathbb Z}\longrightarrow H_1(S;\mathbb F_2)
\]
therefore shows that
\(2(\Gamma'(\Pi\mathbf n)-\Gamma(\mathbf n))\in2A_{\mathbb Z}\). Thus
\(\Gamma'(\Pi\mathbf n)-\Gamma(\mathbf n)\in A_{\mathbb Z}\), and \(\Pi\),
extended by the identity on \(A_{\mathbb Q}\), carries the source lattice
in \eqref{eq:four-negative-integral-gluing} onto the target lattice.

Since all four boundaries are negative, there is an \(F\)-stable
decomposition
\[
 K_{\mathbb Q}=A_{\mathbb Q}\oplus Q_1\oplus Q_2\oplus Q_3\oplus Q_4.
\]
For \(y\in F\), choose one of its two lifts to \(L\) and use it in both
representations. Let \(s_i(y)\in\{1,-1\}\) be its boundary sign on
\(Q_i\). The complement-fixed lifts of \(k_{12}\) and
\(k_{34}\) have sign vectors
\[
 (-1,-1,1,1),
 \qquad (1,1,-1,-1).
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:relative-displacement} then gives
\[
 (-1)^{h_{12}(y)}=s_1(y)s_2(y),
 \qquad
 (-1)^{h_{34}(y)}=s_3(y)s_4(y).
\]
Normalize the source at the unique stop on \(B_1\) and the target at
the unique stop on \(B_2\). Writing \(s_i=s_i(y)\), the source signs are
\(
 (1,s_1s_2,s_1s_3,s_1s_4)\),
while the signs of the target action of \(\alpha(y)\) are
\(
 (s_1s_2,1,s_1s_4,s_1s_3)\). They are intertwined by \(\pi=(12)(34)\). Before normalization, the
central corrections change the boundary-sign vector to
\((s_2,s_1,s_4,s_3)\) and act trivially on \(A_{\mathbb Q}\). The sign
at the target normalization point on \(B_2\) is therefore \(s_1\),
the same sign used to normalize the source at \(B_1\). The two
normalizations multiply the common absolute action by the same sign,
so the normalized absolute actions also agree.

On the non-trivial root blocks, the formulas for \(\phi\) act as the
inverses of the target signed cycles. Signed reversal conjugates each
cycle to its inverse. Together with \(\pi\), these reversals give an
integral intertwiner of the full stabilizer representations. The
ordinary homological action is unchanged, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:normalized-representation-extension} completes the
extension to \(\mathfrak R\).
<!-- end proof-43 -->
